FRANK PALMER MAIZE

FLIGHT'S END Chapter I 

GERDA, wife of the eighth Earl of Bryndham, sitting solidly on one of the side-chairs in Fancine's gray and gold salon, was witnessing her first fashion parade. 

When it began she had clapped vigorously as each proud beauty appeared beneath an arching doorway, but Mrs. 

Edgeworth's look of dismay and Nancy's quick touch on her arm had given her to understand that applause was not necessary. After that she had contented herself by nodding and beaming her approval when the young women languorously descended a short flight of marble steps and came undulating across the room with the dip, sway and glide peculiar to mannequins. In turn they posed and revolved, eyes aloof and expressionless, lips vaguely smiling, then folded expensive wraps about their litheness and floated gracefully away. "Dem is nice-looking girls," her ladyship declared, in a loud aside to Mrs. Edgeworth," but dey got an awful funny valk! Is dat de style now, in New York?"

Mrs. Edgeworth did not hear the question. She was inclining an attentive ear to the valued remarks of Fan-

FLIGHT'S END 

cine, who was speaking authoritatively on the latest Paris silhouette. It was only because Mrs. Edgeworth was an old and valued customer, and had appealed to Fancine for her advice, that she was giving them this quite exceptional share of her personal attention, on the occasion of her formal showing of Spring models. Now, as she talked, her shrewdly appraising gaze rested upon

Lady Bryndham, taking in every detail of her squat, middle-aged figure; the unfashionable hat, the smooth brown hair arranged in a braided mat over each ear, the round face, its high cheek-bones touched with natural color, the wide-set blue eyes and large, humorous mouth. The survey ended with the plump red hands which were uncompromisingly spread upon her ladyship's knees, and the broad feet that protruded, in square-toed shoes, beneath the skirt of her modest black suit.

Mrs. Edgeworth, awaiting Fancine's reaction, noted a slight protruding of the Frenchwoman's lower lip and a faint lifting of her brows, and interpreted these signs to mean that there were limits to the powers even of 

New York's most famous couturière. However, Fancine's brisk nod reassuringly added, one would do one's best. 

What ees eet, exactly, zat her ladyship requires?' she asked. 

Mrs. Edgeworth's smile was graciously confidential. "I really know very little more about her needs than you do. A former classmate of mine, now living in

FLIGHT'S END 

9. 

California, asked me to help Lady Bryndham in the selection of a suitable outfit, while she was in New York. 

I told you when I telephoned, did I not, that she is the wife of the Earl of Bryndham, who has recently inherited the title and a large estate in England? "Oui, madame, I have understood." 

"When I received Mrs. Westcott's letter, asking me to assist Lady Bryndham in choosing whatever she would need, I thought instantly of you. They arrived this morning and we came over here direct from the station, as they have only a few days to spend in New York." "Madame was vairee kind to sink of me!" Fancine murmured. "I am greatlee honored! "

Her tone was obsequious but her eyes were faintly mocking as they watched Lady Bryndham, now excitedly beckoning to a regal creature in a long white evening wrap. "Hi, young lady!" she was calling. "Come back here, please! Ay vant to see dat coat again! 99 The mannequin reversed with mechanical precision. 

A saleswoman, fluttering about her ladyship, gave voice to ecstatic words of praise. "It will be charming for Madame!" she glibly lied. "Such a lovely model! Such graceful lines! Our very latest importa—" Her suave patter ended in a sound that was almost a grunt. Lady Bryndham had bent forward and poked her, amidribs, with a jovial forefinger.

FLIGHT'S END 

"Na, na, miss!" she chided, with a deep, rich chuckle. "You know dat ain't no kind of coat for a fat old Svede like me! Ay vould look like de dickens in dat! It's for Nancy ay vant it. Try dat coat on, Nancy! Ay t'ank you look fine in dat!"

A flicker of surprise crossed Fancine's well-regulated features as the girl who was sitting beside Lady Bryndham rose from her chair and allowed the saleswoman to fold the shimmering garment about her slim young form. 

She had pulled off her hat, and her burnished red-gold hair, short and softly waving, was like a high, gay note of music in the neutral-tinted room. Vividly her reflection flashed back at them from the long mirrors, her piquant face framed by the white fur collar, her dark gray eyes, deeply lashed, dancing with amusement as 

Lady Bryndham loudly clapped her hands. "Hi yi! " exulted her ladyship. "Look at yourself, Nancy! How grand you are! Dat coat you could vear ven you go to call on de Queen!"

Fancine was nodding her own approval. 66 Mais, she ees charmeng, ze daughter!" she exclaimed. "Ze hair, ze eyes, zey are ravissante!" ·CC Miss Westcott is in no way related to Lady Bryndham," explained Mrs. Edgeworth. "She is the daughter of the friend of whom I spoke. Nancy has lately graduated from college and is now going over with the 

Bryndhams, to visit them for a while in their new home.' 

Her ladyship overheard the last words and twisted about to face the Frenchwoman.

FLIGHT'S END 

II "Listen, miss," she said eagerly. "Nancy is going to run around vid de young folks and have a good time, over dere, and ay vant her to have all de different kinds dresses she's going to need." 'Oh, Gerda, only one or two," the girl protested. “I have a trunkful of things, you know." "Yeh, but you vould need more. So go ahead, miss, and fix Nancy up first, and den you could show me somet'ing for myself." 66 

Parfaitement, madame!" agreed Fancine, and with the glow of inspiration on her face went into action. 

For a time the fashion parade exhibited only such styles as were particularly suited to Nancy's type, and 

Lady Bryndham rocked with delight as clothes for travel and for sports, gowns for afternoon and for evening passed in review before them. She had entered upon an orgy of buying, and impatiently waved aside the girl's repeated remonstrances. "Leave me alone, Nancy," she pleaded. "Ay am doing vot ay have alvays vished ay could do, and ay am having de time of my life! "

Her enjoyment ended when the last harmonizing accessory had been chosen for Nancy's wardrobe. Her own requirements it was not so easy to meet. After an intensive half-hour, during which gown after gown had been rejected, her ladyship issued her ultimatum. "You know de old saying, miss," she said, twinkling up at Fancine. "You couldn't make from a sow's ear a silk purse! You vould yost have to fix me up a couple

FLIGHT'S END 

plain black dresses dat's right for a fat old voman like me. In dem grand clothes ay vould only look foolish." 

It was the truth, as the Frenchwoman's intent brow confessed, but in the end she achieved a triumph by designing a model whose felicity of line was subtly flattering, and from which a suit, a dress of black silk and one of lace were to be made with all speed. 

And I doubt," said Mrs. Edgeworth, with finality, "if anyone but Fancine could have done as well, for it takes a real artist to clothe such a figure." 

She had drawn Nancy into a window-seat of the outer salon, while Lady Bryndham was engaged in one of the fitting rooms, and was settling herself for their first confidential talk since their meeting that morning. (6 

Begin at the beginning, my dear!" she commanded. "Of course I'm simply consumed with curiosity, for your mother's note told me almost nothing. How long have you known the Bryndhams and where did you meet them?” 

Nancy was intently studying the stitching of her gloves, her downcast lashes veiling a glint of mischief.

She was wickedly anticipating the effect her announcement would have upon the aristocratic New Yorker, who had received the titled couple at the station with such marked cordiality. "I really can't remember a time when I didn't know

Gerda," she began slowly. "She came to us, as nurse and cook and general helper, soon after I was born, and has been with us ever since."

FLIGHT'S END 

13 Mrs. Edgeworth was staring at her with a shocked expression in her pale blue eyes. 

Your mother wrote me that they were friends of yours!" she said severely. "So they are!" Nancy replied with spirit. "At least - Gerda is. She's just about the best friend we have." "A servant!" Mrs. Edgeworth's lips were wry over 

Of course one can see at a glance that the woman is hopelessly common, but —” the words. (6 "She doesn't seem so to me!" Nancy said with a flash of resentment. "Gerda's been like a second mother to me all my life. She was married when I was about five years old, but even then she wouldn't leave us. 

She's been coming to us, for a part of each day, up to the time that they received news of the inheritance, about three weeks ago." 

And Lord Bryndham? What was he, before he inherited his title? " 

Nancy's eyes were dancing wickedly. This was going to be another shock for the still hopeful social leader. "Jim?" she said casually. "Why, he had a half interest in a little garage, and between times acted as chauffeur for one of our neighbors. He was a very good chauffeur, Miss Kenton said. She felt perfectly safe when Jim was at the wheel of her car." 

Mrs. Edgeworth did not sense the teasing note which almost rippled into laughter. She was sighing over the lowering of the standards of one of England's finest old families.

FLIGHT'S END 

"One can see," she admitted, "that a man might be reduced by circumstances to any kind of work, in such times as these, but that doesn't excuse his taking up with a woman so far beneath him in class; poor, ignorant probably, certainly uncultured, a good deal older than he, altogether the wrong person for him to have married." "We've always thought it was just the other way," 

Nancy said with decision, “that Gerda was too good for Jim." 66 

What do you mean by that?"

Nancy hesitated. The feeling that they had about 

Gerda's husband was based on uncertain grounds. They had no proof, but more than once they had suspected him of certain transactions, such as the buying and selling of stolen cars and other short cuts to increased profits, which indicated a weakness of moral fibre. 

Then, too, he was such a mysterious individual, whose real nature seemed hidden beneath an unnatural reticence. Yet they all liked Jim, even though they could not quite understand Gerda's absolute worship of him. 

I hardly know what I do mean," she confessed. “After all, Jim's been very good to Gerda and she simply adores him. She saved his life, when he was desperately ill and no nurses to be had. And she's been a wonderful wife to him." "Wonderful for a chauffeur, perhaps," Mrs. Edgeworth said with an acid inflection, "but not for the Earl of Bryndham."

FLIGHT'S END 

15 "Perhaps he didn't expect to be an earl. His father had run away from home and been disinherited, long before Jim was born. Jim had never heard from his grandfather until he happened to see an advertisement asking for the whereabouts of the heir to the Bryndham estate." "In any case he should never have married this woman," Mrs. Edgeworth said decisively. "And now he is making a fatal mistake in taking her over to England and presenting her to British society. He should most certainly divorce her before he goes." 

The teasing sparkle had vanished from Nancy's eyes.

She had been looking forward to this meeting with her mother's oldtime friend, had expected to share with her some of her own misgivings when she tried to picture Gerda as the mistress of Bryndham Court; but

Mrs. Edgeworth, with her unfeeling talk of divorce, her scorn of Gerda, had closed a door upon all such confidences. No use trying, Nancy told herself, to explain the goodness of the "impossible Swede," her abounding generosity, her unselfishness, the kindly, humorous philosophy with which she had so often helped the Westcott family to meet the problems of life. The conventional woman of society was not concerned with character, except as associated with wealth and background. For her the only person of consequence in the affair was the Earl of Bryndham, whose cultural deficiencies she would condone because of his money, his title and his connection with a proud old family.

FLIGHT'S END 

"The New York papers had only very slight notices of the discovery of the Bryndham heir," she was saying. "They seemed uncertain as to the size of the estate he had inherited. It's a very large one, is it not?" 

With a quick, impatient movement Nancy opened her handbag and drew out an envelope. "I think these clippings from our local newspapers will give you all the details," she said briefly. "Mother saved them for you."

Better to let her read the accounts for herself, she thought with a touch of irritation, as she studied Mrs. 

Edgeworth's haughty profile. It was the size of the estate in which she was interested, and not the personal element of the story as Nancy would have recounted it. 

Never would she forget the dramatic moment when

Gerda had come bursting into the Westcott home as they sat at the breakfast-table, carrying a red-sealed document in her hands and excitedly shouting: "My Yim is a lord! His grandfadder dies and leaves him a handle to his name, a lot of money, a house in England vid fifty rooms, and t'ree t'ousand acres of land!" 

Nancy and her pretty mother and her professor-father had all stared unbelievingly at the woman who stood there before them, wearing a skirt that had belonged to Mrs. Westcott, a blouse made from one of Nancy's last-season dresses, and incongruously talking of titles and riches. It was as though one of Gerda's own fat pigeons had suddenly flaunted the gorgeous plumage

FLIGHT'S END 

17 of a peacock, or her sturdy geraniums had flamed into exotic bloom. 

But the astonishing story was true, as the red-sealed document and Jim's birth-certificate had proved. The next day the papers of the little Southern California university town had featured it with the staring headlines and the double columns which Mrs. Edgeworth was now scanning. Reporters, coming in search of 

Gerda, had swarmed about Nancy's home, had followed Professor Westcott even into the sacred precincts of his classroom, in hope of additional news, had flared unexpected flashlights into the startled face of Mr. Carstairs, the elderly English solicitor who had come over to represent the Bryndham estate, and had even photographed Gerda in the act of hanging the Westcott family washing on the line. That absurd picture, and one of the 

Earl of Bryndham, grease-stained and clad in overalls and jumper, accompanied the account which was now in Mrs. Edgeworth's hands. From her corner of the window-seat Nancy could read the screaming headlines which ran across the entire width of the page: 

"CHAUFFEUR INHERITS ENGLISH TITLE AND GREAT ESTATE" 

Below was a two-column heading: "Advertisement in newspaper wrapped around new automobile tire brings tidings of inheritance to local heir "

FLIGHT'S END 

At great length followed the story of Jim's father, who at sixteen years of age had left his ancestral home in England, in search of adventure, earned his passage to the United States and made his way to one of the goldmining camps of South Dakota.

Nancy could almost feel the chill of Mrs. Edgeworth's disapproval as she read of the marriage of the eldest son of the seventh Earl of Bryndham to a beautiful 

Mexican girl, who was bar-maid and dancer in one of the camp saloons. "It was the child of that strange union," the article continued, "born in a lean-to in the little mining town, who was later to inherit the estate which his father had forfeited. Orphaned by the killing of James Bryndham in a mine accident, and by the subsequent death of his 

Mexican mother, the boy drifted from camp to camp and finally reached the Western Coast." 

Mrs. Edgeworth's eyes skipped with disdainful haste over the lines which told of Jim's long and faithful service in the employ of one of the leading citizens of the town, and his marriage to Miss Gerda Nillson, "a valued employee in the home of Professor Homer Westcott, 1698 Orange Drive." But above the description of the estate she lingered appreciatively, reading some of the items aloud as if to savor them more fully. "An Elizabethan manor-house and park, 3200 acres of land, numerous farm buildings, cottages comprising the Village of Bryndham; also extensive holdings in the North of Scotland.' An estate which might be very

FLIGHT'S END 

19 much of a white elephant, in these days, if the late earl had not also possessed a large personal fortune, which is lucky for the husband of your cook! "

She folded the clippings and handed them back to 

Nancy with an ironic smile. "I suppose it's an opportunity for you to see a bit of the world, my dear," she commented, "but I really don't see how you can do it." "Do what?" Nancy asked. "Pardon me if I speak too frankly, but I don't understand how your mother's daughter can bring herself to accept favors from a former servant, live with her on terms of equality and step down to her level in the estimation of others." 

Nancy's chin tilted an indignant inch. "I thought

Mother had explained to you that I am going to earn everything, clothing, travel and all." 

Mrs. Edgeworth's smile was scornful. "Oh, I see!

In the capacity of social secretary to your 'Martha-bythe-day "?" "You might call it that," Nancy said quietly. "It will be difficult for her, at first, and I can help in lots of ways. More than that, I want to stand between

Gerda and the unfavorable impressions people will receive, and try to make them like her. They will, when they really know her, for she's one of the dearest persons that ever lived." 

For an instant words failed Mrs. Edgeworth. "And you belong to the generation which is supposed to be

FLIGHT'S END 

free from idealism and sentimentality!" she scoffed. "Really, Nancy, that's the most fantastic notion I ever heard! Your Gerda will naturally be received in certain quarters, because of her husband's station, but if you expect to make British people of the upper class appreciate' her, you've chosen a particularly solid windmill to tilt against! " "That makes it all the more interesting, doesn't it? " 

Nancy said with a light laugh. 

She stood up quickly, thankful that Gerda was emerging from the fitting-room and that the argument was ended. One could surely believe in the fundamental fair-mindedness of human nature, she thought mutinously, without being sentimental; and since character was the most important thing in life why was it fantastic" to hope that Gerda's new associates might learn to recognize her worth? 

Nancy's face was alight with purpose as she followed the other women down to Mrs. Edgeworth's waiting car. 

British society, even as viewed through the New Yorker's reverent eyes, had no terrors for her at that moment.

An hour later, in a fashionable hotel-restaurant near the Plaza, the first really disturbing doubt had entered Nancy's mind. It had come simultaneously with Mrs. 

Edgeworth's look of utter horror as Gerda made a mélange of the chicken-paté, peas and jellied salad on her plate, and proceeded to eat the rather unappetizinglooking mixture with a vigorous use of her knife. 

It was a procedure with which Nancy had been fa-

FLIGHT'S END 

21 miliar since childhood days. She had often perched beside the kitchen table while Gerda ate, watching her and listening as she told fairy stories between bites. 

Nancy had even, being of an imitative turn of mind, adopted some of Gerda's manners, with distinctly unpleasant results. Gerda's ways, she had been informed, were different, and neither to be criticized nor copied. 

On the recent journey across the country, Gerda had refused to walk the swaying length of several coaches to reach the diner, and trays of food had been sent to her compartment. This was actually, Nancy realized with surprise, the first meal at which she and Gerda had sat down together. Fastidious herself, she was acutely conscious of Mrs. Edgeworth's discomfiture, and was, in a measure, sharing it. Unquestionably the training of Lady Bryndham must begin with a course in table etiquette.

Her ladyship was surveying the room as she ate, her twinkling eyes studying the chattering crowd, the hurrying waiters and the decorative mural paintings. "Ain't it nice here?" she said with warm appreciation. "Vid all dem sea pitchers on de valls ay feel like ay am on a boat. Ay only hope de real vater will be as still, ven ve are on it! 

Mrs. Edgeworth's conventionality would not quite permit her to disregard her duties as hostess. She turned her cool, detached gaze on Gerda as she asked: "You do not enjoy ocean travel, then?"

Lady Bryndham rested her knife in an upright posi-

FLIGHT'S END 

tion on the edge of the table and thoughtfully contemplated an impressionistic sea. "Ay like it all right ven it ain't rough, but ven de ship rocks! Ack, vot dat does to me! Coming over from de Old Country ay vos flat on my back de whole time. Yost ven ay vould feel a little better de voman in de bunk over me vould get sick to her stummick, and ven ay hear her ay vould start in again! Pfui, dat vos terrible! " 

With appalled haste Mrs. Edgeworth changed the subject, but while she questioned Nancy concerning her father's university work and her mother's social activities, she was casting anguished glances at Lady Bryndham, who had poured her tea into her saucer, dipped a cube of sugar into it and was nibbling enjoyably, between prolonged, sibilant sips. "Nancy's ma is a grand lady,” she volunteered, when there was a pause. "She does so much for de poor and don't vaste her whole time contracting, like some vimmin. It's funny dat dey like so much dat game. Ay sooner do a veek's vashing dan play cards. Ven de vashing is done de line hangs full of nice clean clothes, but ven de game is finished you got only a headache and maybe a prize dat you ain't got no use for."

In sympathy for Mrs. Edgeworth's agony, Nancy hastily took the conversational lead from Gerda. (6 

Mother sent you her latest photograph," she said, "to show you the ravages of time. Those are her words," she added with a laugh. "Dad says she's

FLIGHT'S END 

23 changed very little, really, except that her hair is slightly gray." "And she don't care if it is!" Gerda added approvingly. "She don't run to de beauty shoppies, trying to look like sixteen. Ay knew vonce a voman," she went on, with a deep chuckle, “dat dyed her hair and it turned bright green. Sitting in de back yard, drying it in de sun, she feels somet'ing pulling and it's deir old goat! He t'anks her hair is grass and he is trying to eat it! 

She leaned back in her chair, her plump sides shaking with laughter. There were sounds of suppressed mirth from adjacent tables, but Mrs. Edgworth's face showed no answering gleam. Her look was tragic as she swept her party out of the restaurant, before Lady Bryndham had quite finished mopping up the last of her ice cream with a bit of cake.

On their way to the car Mrs. Edgeworth took advantage of a brief separation from Gerda to tell Nancy a few heartfelt truths. "I've had as much of this dreadful person as I can stand!" she said bitingly. "I'll give you a list of shops and send the car to your hotel each morning while you are in New York, but I positively can do no more. Worst of all, I had invited some friends to meet her at my club, this afternoon, but of course I can't go on with it. I do think, my dear, that your mother might have told me what to expect. It was most thoughtless of her to neglect to do so!"

FLIGHT'S END 

Anxious furrows appeared on Mrs. Edgeworth's brow as she pondered the most plausible excuse for the non-appearance of her titled guest. She might tell her friends that dear Lady Bryndham had been greatly fatigued by the journey, and the earl did not think it wise further to tax her strength. But these were all women who were as much at home in England as in their own country. Sooner or later they would hear the story of the Swedish cook who had become mistress of Bryndham Court. Perhaps it would be wiser to tell them the truth, make an amusing tale of it. . . .

Nancy was also thoughtful, as the car whirled away with them after depositing Mrs. Edgeworth at her club. 

The luncheon hour had been an ordeal for her, as well as for their hostess. She had flushed hotly when she met the supercilious eyes of the women at the other tables, and had hated herself for being ashamed of Gerda. But it had given her a new understanding of the difficulties that lay before her. Gerda was sweettempered but her habits were fixed, and it would not be easy to break them. Meanwhile there would be other embarrassing incidents, other distressing situations

Gerda, who had been gazing out into the streets, taking in the sights and sounds of the city, turned from the window with a sigh.

In such a big town," she said, "dere must be an awful lot of poor folks. You know, ay t'ank if ay lived here ay vould have alvays a heartache, because all

FLIGHT'S END 

25 around of me ay vould feel trouble and suffering." 

Her round face was solemn as she looked at Nancy. "Did you notice de little girl dat vaited on our table?" she asked. "So t'in and vite she vos, and so sad!

Eider she is sick or she's got some hard luck. Ay put a dollar in her hand ven ve come avay, and ven she smiled at me, Nancy, de poor little t'ing had tears in her eyes! 

"You see?" Nancy's thought fiercely challenged. "That's the kind of thing Gerda does while you sit and sneer! You never even saw the girl she's talking about!"

It was herself that she accused. Already, under the excitement of preparation for the voyage and anticipation of experiences to come, she was forgetting that Gerda was her Job. She had set out to reveal a particularly lovable and kindly nature to eyes which might otherwise see only surface crudities. Perhaps it would be difficult; perhaps she had chosen a "solid windmill to tilt against," but if she should succeed, as she was resolutely determined to do, it would be an achievement worthy of her effort.

Chapter II 

THE great liner was moving out into the harbor. 

Already the high-flung pinnacles of New York had receded until they appeared to float like a pearly mirage in the twilight haze. Drawing away from the fussy tugs, squat ferry-boats and smaller craft, the ship was powerfully increasing its speed. 66 

Nancy was standing quietly beside Gerda at the rail, but she was anything but quiet in spirit. She was filled with a wild desire to fling her arms above her head and shout an exultant hail to the crested, beckoning waves beyond the Narrows. Near her was a group of seasoned travelers, speaking casually of their numerous crossings "; but to her it was all fresh and untried, and the realization, moreover, of a lifelong dream.

Some day, she had always told herself, she would sail over far seas to distant lands. Now the dream had taken on the enchanting shape of reality, and neither Mrs. 

Edgeworth's dark prophecies nor her own vague doubts could dim its glamour.

She smiled as she thought of the New Yorker's pessimistic parting words: "I give you about three weeks to tire of your experiment, my dear," she had said. "You young people are

FLIGHT'S END 

27 forever looking for 'thrills,' but you certainly will not find them in the Bryndhams' country manor-house. 

Stop off on your way home, for a visit with me, and 

I'll give you a really good time."

She had meant to be kind. She had arranged an evening at the opera,- after making quite certain that Gerda did not wish to go, and had been very gracious to Jim. But when she had seen them all aboard the ship she had hurried thankfully away, and there had been no familiar face in the throng which had gathered at the dock. That was the first homesick moment 

Nancy had known, and to relieve it she had picked out one unsuspecting man, whose thin, scholarly face and graying hair reminded her of her father, and had waved persistently at him until dock and crowd were lost in the distance.

Jim was now taking himself off, followed by the elderly solicitor, in search of the cardroom, where they would continue a game begun on the train. Wearing one of his new suits and a light felt hat, Jim was looking unnaturally neat and trim, but his lank figure still had its accustomed slouching, hand-in-pockets posture, the heavy lock of straight black hair which never could be quite subdued had fallen over his forehead, and his old black pipe was in his mouth.

He was assuming his new rôle with a certain nonchalance, but he had winked at Nancy, with the sparkle of humor which he occasionally displayed, when Mr. 

Carstairs had addressed him as "your lordship." Jim

FLIGHT'S END 

would still be Jim, quiet, secretive, his dark, hollowcheeked face inscrutable, his black eyes - lustrous, Mexican eyes regarding the world with a curiously watchful, wary glint; but he would be able to adapt himself, far more readily than his wife, to changed conditions. 

Gerda, who shared neither Jim's mild curiosity nor

Nancy's eager anticipation, on crossing the threshold of the unknown, was looking wistfully back at the lights which were beginning to prick out through the dusk. 

Their faint beams lay reflected in glinting golden threads across the water, forming the last faint link between ship and shore.

She gave a long, gusty sigh as Nancy linked a sympathetic arm through hers. Ay vos yost vishing," she said gloomily, "dat ve have never got dis money. 

Dere is an old saying: ' Money makes not so much happiness as trouble.' And it's true, Nancy! In my heart is somet'ing dat tells me it is better ve should not go." 

"Now you're borrowing trouble! Nancy reproached. "That isn't a bit like you, Gerda." "But it's how ay feel. Ay vould give all the money, de big house, de land, de whole of England-for our little bungalow vid de pretty purple wine growing over de front porch! 

For a moment Nancy visualized, as clearly as Gerda, that tiny house at the foot of their neighbor's garden, half hidden beneath a rioting bouganvilla vine. She

FLIGHT'S END 

29 knew every one of the bright chromos that lined its walls, the innumerable "tidies" crocheted by Gerda's clever hands, the pattern of the beaded lambrequin which hung from the mantel and the colors of the wax flowers which bloomed perpetually beneath a round glass dome. She had struck the match for the burning of the mortgage on the little home and had helped in the selection of many of the furnishings which had been added from time to time. On the latest of their purchases, a lordly davenport which half filled the tiny livingroom, Gerda had made the final payment the very day before the English solicitor had come stepping down the path between her rows of calla lilies, bringing the news which had disrupted her life.

One by one the golden threads of light were breaking. When the last of them was severed Gerda turned with an air of finality from the rail. "So!" she said. "It is finished. Vot is done is done and ay vouldn't grunt no more, so don't vorry about me, Nancy. Vot ay vant most of all is dat you have a good time." 

That wish, that Nancy should have a good time, was like a leitmotiv which was continually being sounded by Gerda, either from her cabin or from the deck-chair in which she was installed on fair days. To the cabin or to the chair all her meals were taken, for she would not trust herself to the uncertain footing of the decks. "Flat on her back," her eyes carefully avoiding the disturbing rise and fall of the horizon, she philosophi-

FLIGHT'S END 

cally endured the voyage, taking her enjoyment vicariously through the "good times" of others.

To Nancy it was unending joy just to be part of the life of the great liner. Her own suite, the pretty, 

chintz-hung cabin, with its brass bed and dainty furnishings; the bathroom, in which an attentive stewardess prepared invigorating baths of pale-green seawater, was her introduction to the luxury of modern sea travel. The first morning out she discovered the other wonders of the floating hotel, the magnificent lounge, the writing-rooms and library, dining salons, both public and private, a well-equipped gymnasium, an enticing green-tiled plunge and even a row of little shops in which a bit of the mode and glitter of Fifth Avenue had been transplanted to the ocean lane. 

Almost at once she was drawn into the activities of a group of gay young people who were lining up sides in preparation for sports finals at the end of the run. 

Deck-tennis, shuffleboard and swimming contests filled the daylight hours, and in the evening there was dancing or motion pictures.

Jim and Mr. Carstairs, who had joined two other men in a foursome at cards, spent most of their time in the club-room, and the deck-chairs which bore their names were more often occupied by some of the young men who came back with Nancy, between games, to chat with Gerda. 31 

known to his friends as "Chubby," was her favorite.

He was a reporter on one of the New York dailies. 

At least, as he confided blithely, he would have that enviable position when he returned from his travel jaunt with his father and mother. His "nose for news had been the justification for taking up a journalistic career. He had worked for a year, after graduation from college, on a Cleveland paper, and now, at last, was to be given his chance in the metropolis. 

Seated on the foot-rest of Gerda's chair, he amused her with his tales of news-hunts and of "scoops," but up to the fifth day the boasted reportorial instinct had been dormant and Chubby had failed to identify Jim and Gerda, named on the passenger list as "Mr. and 

Mrs. J. Bryndham," with the recently discovered heir to an English estate. It was a chance remark of Gerda's which aroused his suspicion and sent him hot-foot along the trail.

He had been talking of Westminster Abbey, which he had visited on a former trip to London, and of the illustrious names upon its pavements and tablets. "It made me kind of sick," he said, " to see people walking so indifferently over those graves, with hardly a thought for the famous warriors and statesmen and writers who lie buried there. I suppose it's all right, but it certainly seemed like sacrilege to me."

Nancy smiled as she watched Chubby's eagerly listening face. It was clever to give Gerda that lead, start her reminiscences with her own romance. There was a far-away look in her eyes as she began her narrative with the winter when an epidemic of influenza had raged in the university town. Hospitals were filled and the demand for nurses far exceeded the supply. Jim, lying dangerously ill in the chauffeur's cottage at the foot of a neighboring garden, had been taken under Gerda's kindly wing and by her persistent, devoted care his life had been saved. A few months later, on Nancy's fifth birthday, they were married in the little bungalow which Gerda had cleaned until it glittered and somewhat funereally decorated with tall white calla lilies. "Sixteen years de tventy-first of Yune it will be, since ve vos married," Gerda said dreamily, "and nobody couldn't be happier dan ve have been, all dat time."

Mr. Maudsley was an impressive old gentleman, short and very thin but tremendously dignified and ceremonious. He would have been astonished to know, as he came forward, erect, sartorially elegant, gray spats twinkling, monocle gleaming, that to Nancy he was England incarnate, welcoming her to whatever new and delightful and thrilling experiences it had in store for her. He did not at first notice the American girl, particularly, his attention being fixed on Jim and Gerda. 

He gave each, in turn, as he shook hands with them, a long, searching look, and Nancy saw the quick, involuntary lifting of the surprised white tufts of hair which were his eyebrows. "It is indeed a great pleasure to meet your lordship and Lady Bryndham," he was saying, in a precise, formal manner. "I trust your ladyship stood the voyage well? "

Gerda, smiling and nodding genially, did not reply until Jim nudged her. Then she twinkled at the old man as she explained: "You have to excuse me if ay don't answer ven you use dat name! Ay ain't used yet to being a lady! "

Nancy responded mechanically to Mr. Carstairs' farewells, as he hurried away to take the London train. 

She was thinking uneasily of the position into which Gerda's absurd story had put her. It was distressing to enter upon these new scenes under false colors. She abhorred deception, and had felt like a criminal under the questioning eyes of the two solicitors.

Meantime, this was an English sky which gleamed, pale-blue and cloudless, above her; that was unmistakably a Cockney voice which was shouting orders from a baggage van; and yonder, helmet-strap under chin, white-gauntletted arm majestically raised, was a real 

English "bobby."

She was in one of the lands of her dreams. Her adventure was beginning.

Chapter III 

SPEEDING along the highways of rural England in a limousine of luxurious length and expensive smoothness of action, with a chauffeur in dark-green livery at the wheel, Lord Bryndham and his party were nearing their destination.

Gerda had been interested, at first, in the elegance of the car, which Mr. Maudsley had selected subject to his lordship's approval, and had exclaimed over the softness of its cushions, which she likened to a " fedder bed." But she was now oblivious of everything but the imminent danger of collision. In vain Mr. Maudsley reassured her, explaining the British custom of keeping to the left of the road. She continued to cower in her corner of the seat, wincing in nervous apprehension as each successive automobile or bus bore down upon them, apparently to certain death.

Looking out eagerly upon Springtime England, Nancy had found it, for a time, distressingly flat and uninteresting; but presently they came into a section where the countryside was gently billowing, and lush greenness lay like a velvet robe. Lilacs nodded over high hedges, white candles of horse-chestnuts were lifted high, and here and there, nestled cozily among

FLIGHT'S END 

45 trees which Corot would have loved to paint, was a village with satisfyingly quaint houses, even an occasional one with a high, thatched roof. 

There were inns with intriguing names, "The 

Three Peddlers," "The Green Dragon," "The White 

Swan." Pretty cottages stood out sociably beside the road, and there were glimpses of mansions proudly withdrawn beyond their encircling walls. Many of these, Mr. Maudsley said, were empty, with grounds uncared-for and farm buildings falling into decay. "The day of the landed estate is almost over," he declared, a note of sadness in his voice. "They are no longer self-supporting, and with incomes from other sources greatly decreased many owners are unable to meet the cost of maintenance." "How does it come we're not in the same boat?" 

Jim asked. "Some would say, your lordship, that it is because of the proverbial luck of the Bryndhams, but the truth is that your grandfather's investments were extremely conservative and happened to be less affected than others by the financial storms of recent years. Of course there have been losses. Income taxes and death duties have been heavy and certain retrenchments have been necessary. But the earl's fortune was a considerable one, and we have been able to keep the estate running in fairly normal condition."

Nancy was beginning to understand, as the old solicitor talked on, something of the patriarchal relationship

FLIGHT'S END 

that had existed between these lords of the manor and their retainers. They had given them employment on their farms and in their forests, looked after their welfare, decided their disputes and pensioned them when they were old. This was the seignorial responsibility which to a certain extent still existed on the Bryndham estate, and to which Jim had fallen heir, together with the earl's fortune and title. Perhaps he was realizing it, for Nancy thought she saw a shadow of uneasiness cross his face.

Meticulously fulfilling his duties as guide, Mr. 

Maudsley from time to time lifted a neatly gloved hand to indicate points of interest. As they came closer to 

Bryndham Court he mentioned names of owners of the houses they were passing. "That large place on the hill," he said, "is Wilderspin Castle, where Sir Pembroke Nairne lives, with his sister and two sons, the elder of whom is a Member of Parliament. At your right is the studio of Miss Alys 

St. George, a very famous portrait painter. Next is 

Mrs. Coxe-Reed's residence, Sainsbury Park, and to the left, through the trees, you will see Athelstane 

Manor, the home of your nearest neighbor, Lady 

Victoria Thwaite, whose land marches with yours.

Lady Thwaite was one of the closest friends of the late 

Lady Gwyneth Bryndham, your lordship's aunt."

Gerda was silent, gazing out at the imposing houses. 

Jim sat with folded arms, as if unconcerned, but his calmness was a thin veneer which did not quite cover

FLIGHT'S END 

47 his excitement. He was very pale and his eyes, roving from side to side, had a wild, almost fearful glint in their dark depths.

Mr. Maudsley was also slightly nervous. He adjusted his monocle, cleared his throat impressively. (6 'In a few moments," he said, "we shall enter upon your lordship's land. When we cross the bridge we shall be in Bryndham Village."

The car sped over an arching stone bridge, beside which was an old mill, and along a street of the most picturesque cottages Nancy had ever seen. They were steep-roofed stone houses, so mellowed with age that they seemed, like the trees that sheltered them, to have grown where they stood. They formed a long single row, facing a little river, and were joined together in sets of two or three, with a high-pointed gable above each front door. Every one had its low stone fence enclosing a tiny garden, where pink tulips lifted like tall chalices out of beds of bright blue forget-me-nots. 

Bryndham Village," said Mr. Maudsley, "is one of the most beautiful in England, and one of the few which has not been spoiled by the encroachment of modernity."

A woman standing in one of the cottage doorways waved as they passed, and faces appeared in some of the windows. The car edged a village green, in the center of which was a huge oak, passed an old church with a Norman tower and followed a road bordered on one side by a high stone wall.

FLIGHT'S END 

"This wall encloses the grounds of Bryndham Court, your lordship," Mr. Maudsley announced. "On ahead you can see the towers of the entrance gate."

The car slowed, turned under a majestic stone arch between the towers. A man who had swung open the high iron gates stood back and touched his cap.

Gerda was peering out curiously. Just inside the wall was a comfortable-looking two story house, at sight of which she gave a cry of pleased surprise. "Vy, it ain't so big! And it's red brick! Ay alvays like a red brick house. See, Yim! It's kinda nice, ain't it?"

She was reaching for the handle of the door, ready to alight, but the car was flashing on, leaving the red brick house behind them, and Mr. Maudsley was explaining that it was the Lodge, where the estate steward lived. 

Nancy struggled with a desire to laugh as she looked at the chauffeur's green-liveried back. She knew he had heard, for the back expressed shocked disdain in every rigid line.

Now they were winding along an avenue of magnificent elms, whose branches formed an arching roof high overhead. On either side were vistas of parkland, whose venerable trees were like lofty columns, widely spaced. On they went, always up a slight grade, swept out at last into the open, circled a wide stretch of closeclipped turf and formal gardens, and stopped before the lowest of a series of wide stone steps which led up

FLIGHT'S END 

49 from one balustraded terrace to another. Beyond the highest of them rose the stately gray walls of Bryndham Court. 

To Nancy the beautiful house made instantaneous appeal. It had the look of peace and tranquillity which belongs to homes that have been long lived in and greatly loved. A mantle of ivy partly covered it, reached to the top of one of the third-story gables and onto the gray stone slates of the roof. A gentle breeze was rippling the multitudinous leaves, and it seemed to her, for a moment, as though the proud old mansion stirred with life at the coming of its new master. 

Gerda had stood, staring speechlessly, after Mr.

Maudsley had helped her from the car. 

When they started up the terrace steps she caught at Nancy's hand and clung to it, her wide, awed gaze fixed on the house. 

Through the hand she was holding Nancy could feel her agitated tremors and was conscious of her own accelerated heart-beats. Anyone, she told herself, would be thrilled by the drama of that moment. "You will notice," Mr. Maudsley was saying as they mounted, "that the house is built in the shape of the letter 'E,' a form frequently employed by the architects of the Elizabethan period as a delicate compliment to their queen. And the central one of those heads with wing-ruff collars, above the porch," he added, pointing with his cane, "is supposed to be a likeness of 

Queen Elizabeth."

51 It was Mrs. Sheppard who made the address of welcome, her white-pompadoured head held high. My niece," Gerda faintly interpolated. "And your ladyship's niece," the housekeeper corrected herself, "to the house which has so long been waiting for your coming."

There was an awkward pause when she ended her remarks. Mr. Maudsley was whispering to Jim, and after a moment, as though impelled by the old man's exigency, Jim began haltingly to speak. The unruly black lock had fallen over his forehead, and like a schoolboy about to recite a piece he stood with eyes downcast, turning his hat over and over in his hands. "I-ah-just want to say," he mumbled, "that we ah appreciate your-ah-kind words, and ah hope-we're-all-going-to-get-along-fine-together." we 

As he rushed his response to a close there was a polite, appreciative murmur and then all eyes turned on Gerda. Nancy had been conscious, while Jim talked, of critical glances which swept Lady Bryndham from head to foot. Now they were all waiting, their atten-

FLIGHT'S END 

tion fixed so pointedly upon her that she did not need Mr. Maudsley's encouraging bow nor Jim's commanding nod to tell her that something was expected of the wife of the new earl. She swallowed hard, rubbed her nose, clasped and unclasped her hands, and finally made a hesitating start: 

"You you vould have to excuse me, because ay don't know how to make no speech, but ay-ay am pleased to meet you and ay-ay- 

She floundered and stopped. An uncontrollable titter broke from one of the younger housemaids, to be sternly suppressed by a glare from the housekeeper. 

Gerda turned her head and looked back at Nancy, her eyes round and troubled as those of a frightened child. It was a poignant appeal and one which Nancy could not resist. She took a step forward and stood beside Gerda. "My aunt is tired and a little confused," she said quietly. "Perhaps she will let me express her gratification at finding installed, in her new home, a staff which is ready to carry on all the old traditions of loyalty and of willing service."

This new voice, young though it was, held a note of authority which the servants were quick to recognize. 

Whatever the new mistress of Bryndham Court might be, her niece was unmistakably a lady.

In her relief at having been rescued from the ordeal of speech-making, Gerda was pumping Mrs. Sheppard's hand, and would have gone on down the line to shake

FLIGHT'S END 

53 hands with the others; but they were dispersing with respectful quietness. Jim was starting away with Mr.

Maudsley and Captain Coombes to inspect the grounds, and the housekeeper was waiting to show her ladyship and her niece to their rooms. 

As they crossed the low-ceiled, spacious hall, Nancy looked about with keen interest. It was panelled, beamed and floored with time-blackened oak. On the walls were portraits, groups of swords with points centered upon battle-scarred breastplates, coats of arms and escutcheons. On either side were doors opening into rooms where there was sunshine and color, but the hall was dim and shadowy, lighted only by one high, narrow window above the stairway landing. 

She had stopped to look at a date above the great stone fireplace when she felt Gerda's hand clutching her arm. "Do you know vot dat is?" her ladyship whispered, her eyes on a suit of medieval armor which stood up on a tall pedestal. "It's von of dem cases dey keep mummy-skeletons in! Ay seen a lot of dem in dat museum in New York. Ay bet dey got some dead relation of Yim's in dere! "

There was no time to explain. The housekeeper led them on, up the wide oaken stairway and into the boudoir of the suite which, she said, had always been used by the lord and lady of the manor. It was a gracious room, and Nancy saw Gerda shake her head as she caught an unexpected glimpse of her own squat

FLIGHT'S END 

form, reflected in an ebony-framed pier-glass. The walls were hung with mauve damask. Chairs, stools and sofa were covered with the same material, for which the dark purple of velvet rugs and draperies formed a rich background. Lace curtains swept from ceiling to floor at the windows, framing between their folds a charming view of terraces and garden.

A former dressing-room, which had been converted into a bathroom of glittering tiles, led to an inner chamber in which stood a vast state bed, canopied by elaborate scrolls and ancient draperies. Gerda stopped on the threshold of that room, and flung her hands above her head. "Hi yi!" she marvelled. "Vot a bed! It's big enough for a whole family! 

"It is a bed," the housekeeper proudly explained, in which royalty once slept, during a visit to Bryndham Court a century or more ago. The rooms which have been arranged for your ladyship's niece were used, on that occasion, by a young princess, and ever since then they have been called 'the Princess Suite.'"

For the first time Gerda displayed active interest. "Oh, you fix de 'princess rooms' for Nancy?" she beamed. "Dat's fine! Ay like to see dose rooms."

Marching along between the rows of portraits which lined the corridors, she cast wondering glances at young women in sweeping robes, dowagers in velvet and diadems, men in courtly garb, lace ruffles at their sleeves. 

Sensing the pathos of Gerda's trudging figure, beneath

FLIGHT'S END 

55 that battery of eyes, Nancy put a protective arm about her shoulders, and absurdly glared back at a haughty 

Earl of Bryndham in a Henry VIII costume, with corslet and ruff. 

Gerda gave a nod of satisfaction as she entered the boudoir of the "Princess Suite." It was low and wide, with creamy panelled walls and a moss-green velvet carpet. The furniture was covered with old-rose satin whose embroidered flowers had faded to pale ghosts of their once gay selves. Before an exquisite marble chimneypiece was a chaise longue and a table laden with books. On the rack of an ancient spinet was an open folder of music, as if the Lady Gwyneth, who had last occupied the room, had but lately touched the yellowed ivory keys. 

Gerda went on through the suite, to exclaim over the height of the bed and the quaintness of its chintz canopy, but Nancy stood entranced before one of the windows of the boudoir, rejoicing in the beauty of the view she was to have. 

Down below was a plushy lawn, dropping in a succession of gentle slopes to a swift-flowing river. It was a tiny stream, not more than a few yards wide, but it went hurrying importantly on its way, reflecting in its clear water the tones of beech and ash and hazel trees. 

Beyond it were peaceful green meadows, brushed with the gold of buttercups, stretching away across the 

Cotswold Hills. "This, your ladyship, is the girl who is to be your

personal maid," the housekeeper was saying as Nancy turned from the window. "Duff has not had any experience, but I have been training her, and I think you and your niece will find her quite satisfactory."

FLIGHT'S END 

57 "You're the lady of the manor!" Nancy laughed.

Gerda kicked off her shoes, thudded across to the sofa and sat down hard on it, testing its resilience. "You know vot, Nancy?" she said triumphantly. "Dis couch ain't anyvere near as springy as my new davenport!" 

She stretched out on the maligned sofa and groaned. 

Ay only vish it vos my davenport! Ven ay t'ank of dis big house, dat crowd vaiting for me to make a speech, de eyes of all dem pitchers staring at me from de valls, -ay vish ve could start back dis minute for home! " 

Nancy found a light spread and threw it over her. “ "It's just because you're tired," she told her. "You'll feel better when you're rested. Better try to take a little nap before lunch."

She went out quietly and closed the door. She had not echoed Gerda's wish. She knew perfectly well, as she went back to her beautiful "Princess Suite," that she could ask for nothing better than to live for a time in the old house whose charm she was already beginning to feel.

Chapter IV 

THE first luncheon at Bryndham Court was served in the great oak-panelled diningroom, Jim in the master's place at the head of the table, Nancy and Mr. 

Maudsley on either side and Gerda, looking uncertain and anxious, in a high-backed chair at the end. Between the attentions of the butler and her effort to remember certain rules of etiquette, recently suggested by Nancy, the meal was not an enjoyable one for her ladyship. She toyed with the food on her plate, studied the elaborate pattern of the plastered ceiling, the carved family crest upon the sideboard and the strip of exquisite lace which ran the length of the polished table. 

From time to time she looked up at the lighted portrait of the late earl, which seemed to dominate the scene. "Dat pitcher is vatching me all de vile," she said at last. "It makes me feel funny." 

Nancy was certain, as she saw the quick, stealthy glance which Jim cast at the stern features of his grandfather, that he was sharing Gerda's "funny feeling," but his tone was nonchalant as he answered: "Don't look at it, if it bothers you."

Gerda adopted his suggestion and turned her questioning gaze upon him, as if he, too, had acquired an

FLIGHT'S END

Dem all had light hair and blue eyes and de men vos awful tall."

Mexican." "Oh shure, shure! " Gerda mildly agreed. "Dem folks from de South is alvays dark-complected.'

Bryndham, the late earl's second son, had some famous hunters in these stables. Now there are only two horses, the one that Captain Coombes uses and the one which was broken to saddle for Lady Gwyneth Bryndham, your lordship's aunt, shortly before she died, last winter." 

That'll be a nice horse for you to ride, Nancy," said Jim. "And there's a roadster in the coach-house, that you can use any time you want it." (6 "Hi, ain't dat fine!" Gerda warmly approved.

First t'ing ay vould do ay vould break my neck, and den my old man has to get him annoder vife!"

Nothing doing for me, either," Jim said, and added with a mocking grin at Nancy, "we'll let our niece do the riding for the family. She's used to horses.” 

Nancy's eyes were dancing. She wondered what Mr.

Maudsley would say if he could see the horses she was "used to," jaded steeds which the university girls had occasionally "hired" by the hour. He would not have looked at her, she was sure, with that new air of interest and admiration.

He was talking now of the friendly relations which had always existed between the Court and the villagers. 

As soon as Lady Bryndham could conveniently do so, it would be well to make the acquaintance of their tenants. "Just call in here and there, in passing, your ladyship," he explained, "and before long you will know them all. Then, of course, it will be simply a matter of attending to the needs of the older people and any who may be ill."

"But you have! " Nancy blithely told her. "You're going to sell them the idea that you're the best lady of the manor Bryndham Village has ever known!

The housekeeper was waiting for them in the hall, for it had been arranged that they should see at least a portion of the house that day. It was almost, Nancy thought, like being escorted through a museum. Mrs. 

Sheppard had a neat explanatory phrase for every treasure which the Bryndham family, in centuries of travel, had collected.

They began with the drawingroom and the three reception-rooms, which opened one into the other like the ante-chambers of a palace and had, despite the formality of their style, a cheerful look. There was warmth and light in the coloring of wall tapestries and rare rugs, in the sparkle of crystal chandeliers, glow of lacquer cabinets and glint of marble statuary, and

FLIGHT'S END 

everywhere a restraint and refinement of taste which was in harmony with the stateliness of the house. 

Overwhelmed by so much splendor, Gerda stared in silence at carved Venetian chairs and ebony tables inlaid with ivory; at a 16th century embroidered screen and an 18th century "courting seat "; but when they came to the beautiful Adam chimneypiece she smacked her hands together and wagged her head from side to side. "All "Vot do you t'ank of dat!" she exclaimed. the vay from de time of Adam dat chimley comes, and it looks yost as good as new! " 

The housekeeper, Nancy thankfully observed, had not heard that comment. She was leading them on, into the beautiful library and the study adjoining it, then back to the hall with the intention of resuming the inspection of the upper floors. But at the foot of the stairway Gerda stopped. "Could ve, please, go first to de kitchen," she pleadingly asked. "Dat's vot ay like most of all to see." 

She had been, up to this moment, an interested but uncomprehending spectator. Now she was entering a realm in which she was qualified to reign, and her step was brisk as she followed Mrs. Sheppard down the hall.

Yet her first glimpse of the kitchen brought forth a grunt of dismay. The room was enormous, stone floored and beamed with dark oak, with enough copper kettles hanging against the walls, it seemed to Gerda, to feed a regiment.

67 to please the grandfather he was so fond of could he settle down to politics and a seat in the House of Lords!" "Of course he couldn't!" Nancy said understandingly. "It would have been like caging an eagle.” "That it would, miss! His heart, as you might say, was in the clouds, so fond he was of the work. There he had his workshop, and there for years he studied and made his plans and even had one airplane built. And he was right to go his own way, for he's proved himself a proper wonder at the flying and made world records that you've no doubt heard of. After he was through at Oxford he joined the Royal Air Force, and now he's Flight Lieutenant and to be made Squadron Leader, they say, before long."

69 "Oh, I'll be pleased to, miss!

Late that afternoon, while Gerda was unpacking and arranging her belongings, Nancy went slowly down the great oak stairway, stopping on the landing to look at the portrait of the "princely Bryndham.' 

She was glad to be alone, to savor undisturbed the fascinating atmosphere of the house. She was beginning to understand something of the significance of continuity, to comprehend the sense of rhythm which century after century of settled occupancy could bestow upon a dwelling. Studying the suit of armor and the quarterings of the battered shields against the walls, she wished she knew the stories they could tell. The Bryndhams,

Mr. Carstairs had said, were a gallant race, who had served their country in every war of their times. 

Hopeful of finding some record of their prowess, Nancy went on into the library, which to her was the most attractive room of the house. Books crowded close, reaching tier above tier to the ceiling, and between the rich colorings of their bindings were panels of soft gray on which Grecian figures were strikingly frescoed in black. Classic forms had been reproduced in chairs and writing-tables, and on the gray marble mantel were some beautiful little bronzes representing Greek athletes. 

How her scholarly father would have revelled in that room, Nancy thought wistfully, as she browsed through

FLIGHT'S END 

ancient volumes, illustrated manuscripts which she knew must be priceless, first editions autographed with the names of famous writers, a collection such as only a book-loving family could have assembled. She was suddenly sorry for the old house. It was a home 

entrusted with the fame and hallowed by the deeds of men," but now unloved. Jim, though he might feel stirrings of pride, was not culturally fitted to appreciate it, and Gerda would joyfully have exchanged its riches for the homely comfort of her little bungalow. 

Nancy gathered an armful of books, choosing those which might be tales of valor and of derring-do, such as the valiant Bryndhams had performed, and settled herself in a deep chair at one of the windows, before which two peacocks were decoratively strutting on a stretch of plushy, blue-green sward.

It was an hour later when Grimsby, the butler, appeared with a large silver tray, which he deposited on a table at her side. "Your tea, miss," he said respectfully, "and a book which Mrs. Sheppard sent to you, with her compliments." 

With interest Nancy surveyed her first English tea.

It was an inviting one, with slices of thin bread-andbutter, some buns which she was sure must be crumpets, a pot of orange marmalade, sliced currant-cake and tiny cherry tarts. She poured tea into one of the fragile china cups, started on a crumpet and opened the old ledger on whose red-lined pages the housekeeper

FLIGHT'S END 

71 had pasted her precious clippings. 

They were written in the terse phraseology of the newspaper correspondent and contained many technical terms which Nancy did not understand, but they were more thrilling than the romance she had been reading. No need to go to fiction for tales of heroism, for here they were, in her own day, pulsing with the breath of life.

There were countrymen of her own who had startled the world with just such feats of gallant courage and fired her ardent enthusiasm, but she had known of them only through the eyes of the press. This young 

English aviator seemed excitingly real, for she was actually living in the home which had been his. 

There were columns of description of Flight Lieutenant Bryndham's flight to Australia with a robot pilot, hailed as an epochal event; others that told of a record-breaking altitude experiment, when he had scored one more brilliant victory over the mystery of space," and still others devoted to the most recent of his "contributions to knowledge," in a plane with special features which he had himself designed. 

Nancy had always considered hero-worship an indication of low mentality, but the tea cooled in her cup and the crumpets lost their crispness while she turned the pages of the old ledger, her cheeks flushed and her eyes shining as she followed the adventures of the English conqueror of the air. She closed the book with a curiously personal feeling of pride in his success. She

FLIGHT'S END 

liked his unassuming modesty, to which frequent reference was made, but was glad his accomplishments had received such unstinted recognition. He had renounced a great inheritance for his work. It was no more than right that scientists and other notables of his country should confer special honors upon him and that the world should pay tribute to his valor and his skill. 

One thing Nancy had already determined to do. She was going, at the first opportunity, in search of the deserted old house which had been Lancelot Bryndham's sanctum; where he had worked and studied and gained the knowledge which had made him famous. A sentimental journey, Mrs. Edgeworth might have called it, but Nancy thought of it as a pilgrimage, such as the least emotional of persons might pay to a place where genius had flowered. A pilgrimage, but flavored with just enough of the unusual and the romantic to give it the thrill which youth is ever seeking. 

---

Chapter V 

It was more than a week later when Nancy took her first ride on the horse which had belonged to Lady 

Gwyneth. As if rejoiced to feel again the light hand of a woman on the reins, Jet arched her black satin neck and executed a series of dainty dancing steps before settling into her long, easy gait. "Pil The morning was a perfect one for such a grimage" as Nancy had planned. The hill-meadows were embroidered with the gold of buttercups and the blue of forget-me-nots; the little river, which came into view here and there, sparkled gaily on its way; sparrows were chattering in all the trees and a lark, swinging upward toward the sky, sent down a shower of exultant silvery song.

The housekeeper had unsuspectingly furnished Nancy with full directions. She had come to her boudoir, one evening, to get the old ledger for which another set of clippings was ready. While they talked of 

Flight Lieutenant Lancelot Bryndham, it had been easy to draw her into a description of the route to Cotesthorpe. "It's in quite an out-of-the-way section, miss," she had said. "One went, I remember, out the Old Coach

Road, which was once the main highway, branched off

FLIGHT'S END 

at an inn which is now a ruin and turned to the left into a wood road which led to the house, that's likely to be a ruin itself, for it's had little care of late years." 

Nancy had made a mental note of those turnings, but there had been no chance, during that first week at Bryndham Court, to follow them. She had been as good as her word. She had kept Gerda so busy that she had not had time to be homesick, and incidentally had enjoyed every minute of it herself. Mrs. Edgeworth had said there would be no fun in living in a country manor-house, but Nancy was realizing that everything they had done had had its charm for her. 

They had begun their exploration, the first day, with the lovely garden at the foot of the terraces. That garden, half encircled by a twenty foot yew hedge, with its flowers and sundial and fountain- a copy of the Fountain of the Sea Horses in Rome-was a romantic spot, to Nancy, because it had been the particular delight of Lady Gwyneth. She could picture her there, walking in its paths, thinking of the lover she had lost, reading the inscription of the ancient sundial: 

TIME FLIES LINES RISE AND SHADOWS FALL LET IT PASS BY LOVE REIGNS FOREVER OVER ALL 

Later, with the steward as guide, they had inspected the extensive glasshouses and the nursery gardens whose

FLIGHT'S END 

75 blossoming trees were espaliered against the walls. 

They had visited the kennels, to be greeted noisily by two pointers, the last of Barrett Bryndham's pack, and walked through the coach-house, in which a row of carriages stood swathed in canvas wrappings. 

When they had been shown the Upper Farm, the 

Lower Farm and Bryndham Wood, they went farther afield; wound through the pretty towns of Fairford and Avening, saw their first English cathedral at 

Gloucester and spent one memorable day among the mellow old college buildings and famous quadrangles of Oxford.

Then, at last, the time had come when the cottage calls should begin, and with the air of a martyr Gerda had allowed herself to be led down the long avenue of elms and across the village green. 

Nancy giggled as she remembered the brilliant idea with which her ladyship had postponed, for a few additional moments, her dreaded ordeal. "Before ve do anyt'ing else," she had piously remarked, “ay like to go to de cemetery vere Yim's people are buried! " "Fraud!" Nancy had reproached. "Honest, Debby! Ay vould! And ay like to see, too, de church vere Yim's fadder goes ven he vos a little boy."

In a reverent hush she had passed through a trellised gate and walked between rows of stones that were age-blackened and encrusted with moss; but the quiet

FLIGHT'S END 

of the churchyard was broken when she came to a monument on which a marble effigy lay, hands crossed on breast, toes pointing to the sky. "O so hemskt!" she yelled, recoiling sharply. "How dat scared me! At first ay t'ought it vos a corpse, vaiting to get buried!"

Slowly she circled the stone, stooping to read the name on its base, and again gave a loud squawk of dismay. "Look, Nancy!" she gasped. "Am ay seeing t'ings, or is it my name dat's on here?"

Gerda's voice had become a wailing lamentation. 

With firmness Nancy had taken her by the arm and propelled her away from "Yeraldine." But even in the pretty little 15th century church, where marble cherubfaces smiled down from the beams and stained-glass windows etched jewelled patterns on the stone floor, her depression had lingered, for there she found marble tablets to the memory of the wives of other earls of Bryndham, telling of their exemplary lives and conversation, their piety and their charity. 

Awed by the perfections of her predecessors, Gerda had been glumly silent as they came out from the church and entered the one long street of the village. 

It was a cheerful street, its single row of steep-roofed houses facing the wide road-way and the tiny river. 

Over some of the gray stone cottages great plumes of lavender wisteria were tumbling. Tulips stood on tiptoe along the narrow paths, and primroses, stocks and wall-flowers mingled in a riot of color. 

At each of the gardens Gerda's step had slowed, and before one of them she stopped, her hand on the low gate, and looked wistfully at the house. "In such a little place," she sighed, "people can live nice! Look, Nancy! Dis one has got curtains yost like mine at home, and rose-yeraniums in de

But she was happier, now that she had found an outlet for her energy, and for a few days, at least, would not watch the housemaids with envious eyes, as they went about their homely tasks. 

Jim, too, had settled into the routine of the new life, and was beginning to devote a part of each morning to conferences with Captain Coombes, on estate matters, and to the answering of letters, with Nancy as secretary. This work she had taken upon herself, despite the protests of Gerda, and was performing it with determined zeal. 

She sang softly as Jet took her, with the swift, smooth pace of the thoroughbred, along the Old Coach Road.

The place had a desolate air, yet Nancy hesitated, her hand on the wooden latch of the gate. She looked up at the high roof of the trees, where even the birds' nests had an ancestral appearance, and studied the convolutions of the name formed by the arch. On one of the uprights, just at the level of her eyes, two words had been carved in the wood. FLIGHT'S END 

Flight's End!" Of course it was Lancelot Brynd ham, she thought with a glow of excitement, who had given that name to Cotesthorpe, the old refuge which had so often been the end of his flights. 

Her interest quickened by that discovery, she opened the gate and walked around the curve of the driveway. 

To her consternation she saw, when it was too late to retreat, that the front door stood wide open, that a magazine lay, face down, on the top step of the porch and that the curtain of an upper window was stirring gently in the breeze. Someone was living in the longdeserted house.

Chapter VI 

NANCY's first impulse was to hurry away before she had been seen; but when she thought of the eyes that might have been watching her as she peered in from the gate and as she approached the house, she decided upon a more dignified course. She would go on up to the door and ask to be directed to the main highway, just as if that had been her intention all the time.

She mounted the porch steps and lifted the ring of the lion's-head knocker. The door opened into a square hall, from the other end of which a flight of stairs branched to right and to left. An ancient clock and a high, narrow-backed chair stood against the wall on one side, a quaintly carved bench on the other. What she saw she took in at a glance, for a young man was dashing down the stairway, three steps at a time, and in an instant was looming up, startlingly tall, before her. He was slim and broad shouldered, with fair hair, rather stern features and candid blue eyes, the bluest eyes, Nancy was thinking, that she had ever seen, but their expression was distinctly unwelcoming. "Good morning," he said gravely. "Good morning," she brightly echoed. "I'm so sorry to disturb you! I just want to ask if this road leads to the main highway."

89 one of those swift flashes of insight which leave no room for doubt, she knew the reason for his presence in that half-ruined house. His patient was Lancelot Bryndham! 

Nancy clutched the handle of her riding-whip so tightly that it bent in her grasp. Lancelot Bryndham was there, at Flight's End, the old place which had so often been his retreat. This was the mysterious refuge to which he had been taken. This was where he was hiding away from a too-attentive public. If she had needed any confirmation of the accuracy of her "hunch," she would have had it in the frigid reception she had received at the hands of the tall young man. He was probably that devoted friend of whom the housekeeper had spoken. "I'm right! I know I'm right!" she told herself. "Lieutenant Bryndham is here, and this watchdog pal of his thinks I'm either a reporter or another heroworshipping fan who has tracked him down! 

That last thought struck home rather uncomfortably.

After all, she was invading their privacy on false pretences; asking for directions which she did not need. 

Remorsefully she thanked "Soberface," who had finished his instructions, and took herself away with apologetic speed. 

She had reached the foot of the porch and was starting down the driveway when from that open second-floor window came a wild, shuddering cry, that crisped her nerves and sent a shiver of fear through her body. She

Nancy was trembling as she went back to the door and stood there, not knowing what to do. She felt herself an intruder, yet could not go away without at least an offer of help. The heart-sickening cry was still ringing in her ears. It was appalling even to think of such suffering, and to know that it was Lancelot Bryndham, the glorious young eagle, who was lying there in pain and anguish, brought a moan of pity to Nancy's lips.

She was still standing in the doorway when the young man came hurrying back to her. "I do hope you were not frightened," he said, looking at her with apprehensive eyes. “I—I waited,” she explained, rather tremulously. "I couldn't bear to go without asking if there's anything I can do."

A flicker of amusement crossed his face. Nancy saw it and bit her lip angrily. Now he was laughing at her. 

He would tell the sick air-hero that another silly girl was on his trail. "I am interested in Lieutenant Bryndham," she told him in her haughtiest tone, "because I have heard so much about him since I came to England, and I am very thankful that the life of so brave a man is to be spared to his country."

With the slightest of bows she turned and ran down the steps. He overtook her promptly, and walked beside her to the road. "You're an American?" he asked. "Yes." "Over on a visit?"

Bryndhams, who had come to take possession of the estate; thinking that the vulgar curiosity of the new occupants of the manor-house had followed the deposed heir even to the bed where he lay suffering.
