Nancy laughed and rode away. He was the real air-man type, she was thinking, -lithe, vigorous, clean-cut, with the look of far distances in his steady blue eyes. At the turn in the road she glanced back and saw that he was still standing beside the gate, watching her, with one hand on the upright which bore the carved name: Flight's End. Somehow those words seemed prophetic, for she could not rid herself of the feeling that Lancelot Bryndham's condition was grave.

Perhaps the old house would be, in truth, the end of the flight for him. 

She stopped when she came to Crossroads Cottage.

She had told Mrs. Kell where she was staying, and now she must beg her not to broadcast that information. It would be just like the garrulous old lady to chatter, to anyone who happened in, about the girl who was visiting at Bryndham Court and had picked an armful of the treacherous white hawthorne. But there was no response when she rapped at the cottage door. She would return the next day, she promised herself, for a conspiratorial cup of tea with Mrs. Kell.

No sooner had she reached her boudoir than she took from her desk the old ledger, to which Mrs. Sheppard had just added a number of later accounts. She wanted to read them with particular reference, now, to Lieutenant Bryndham's flying mate, Radcliffe Dean. He

FLIGHT'S END 

95 and Lancelot, the clippings informed her, had gone to Oxford together, had taken up aeronautics with equal ardor and had joined the Royal Air Force at the same time. One of his own solo flights was described at some length and much credit given him for his work as co-pilot and wireless navigator.

Among the lately inserted columns were some pictures from the rotogravure section of the newspapers, showing aircraft of various types with men grouped before them. Immediately Nancy recognized her new acquaintance, his tall head rising above the others, his eyes gazing seriously out from the photographs. Beside him was always a shorter, darker young man. 

She knew he must be Lancelot Bryndham, though the first paper had by mistake given his name to "Soberface." She turned another page and carefully counted heads. When the second and third attempts brought the same result, Nancy gave a startled squeak and dropped the ledger in her lap. 

"Soberface" was Flight Lieutenant Lancelot Glen denning Bryndham! The man whom she had taken to be Radcliffe Deane was the famous aviator himself!

She knew, now, why his face had been vaguely familiar. 

He was like that portrait of the "princely Bryndham " which hung over the landing in the hall,— tall, rather stern, with the same look of breeding and the same clear, candid expression in the very blue eyes. 

At first her only feeling was of thankfulness that it was not Lancelot Bryndham who lay, nerve-racked and

FLIGHT'S END 

suffering, in that old house. It was not he who had given that anguished cry. He was well, gloriously strong and unharmed. She laughed aloud, thinking of the trick he had played on her, in allowing her to believe that the sick man was Lancelot Bryndham. Then, abruptly, she ceased to laugh. She was remembering the flicker of amusement that had crossed his face when she had spoken ecstatically of the invalid's recovery. 

How absurd he must have thought her! Like a silly schoolgirl, palpitating over the danger of some hero of the screen!

She tossed the ledger onto her desk and curled up moodily in the windowseat. The one comforting reflection was that she need never see him again. She certainly would not ride in that direction another day, as he had suggested. She would go back to see Mrs. 

Kell, and pledge her to secrecy, then simply forget the whole affair.

Having come to that conclusion, she jumped up and opened the door of the high oak wardrobe. She had worn her old riding-suit that day, reserving Fancine's modele equestrienne for some more important occasion. 

She looked it over approvingly. It was very smart, with the tiny close-fitting hat and shiny black boots that made up the costume. Mrs. Kell, who saw so few people on the Old Coach Road, would appreciate its smartness. There was real satisfaction, Nancy told herself as she closed the wardrobe door, in doing something to please and "hearten" such a nice old lady as 

Mrs. Kell.

Chapter VII 

If the kind Mrs. Kell had hoped that the young lady from Bryndham Court would soon "hearten" her with another call, she was doomed to disappointment. The day after that first ride over the Cotswold hills Jim departed for Newmarket, to attend the races, leaving Nancy in charge of the estate correspondence. For the balance of the week, in spite of Gerda's objections, she devoted herself to clerical duties. 

There were letters asking for aid in benevolent projects, begging letters and letters which strove to enlist the interest of the Earl of Bryndham in innumerable investments, inventions and schemes. She was beginning to recognize the "freaks," as Jim called them, which he promptly consigned to the wastebasket. The others were either answered or merely acknowledged, with action deferred until they could be submitted to Mr. 

Maudsley for his advice.

The Friday mail had been unusually heavy, and noon had found Nancy still engaged and completely absorbed in her "office" tasks. She had finished the last of her replies, after lunch, and then, still in working mood, had attacked the new filing system, whose cards were not yet indexed. She was wearing the dark green jersey dress

she had put on that morning, her hair was rumpled and there was a large ink-stain on her right fore-finger, when callers were announced.

Nancy did not mind. She was in need of bolstering words at that moment. With the exception of the old rector, Mr. Outhet, they had met none of the county people, for they had been on some of their orientation trips when Sir Pembroke Nairne, his sister, Faith Nairne and Miss Alys St. George had made their calls. Two girls were standing at one of the windows, watching the peacocks on the lawn, when Nancy entered the library. A tall, heavily-built woman was beside the mantel, examining one of the small bronzes. She wore a rather faded suit of heather-colored tweed and a shapeless gray felt hat, but they did not detract from her air of authority. Every line of her generous figure, the upthrust of her square chin, the quick glancing of her humorous brown eyes, told of an independent, decisive personality and the unquestioning self-assurance of the aristocrat. “I was just telling my daughters about a wager I lost on this bit of bronze," she said, as casually as if she and Nancy had met before. "The earl dated it a few hundred years earlier than I did, and as usual he proved I was wrong!" 

Her voice was hearty and her hand gave Nancy's a cordial grip. "How do you do, my dear! You're Lady Bryndham's niece, of course. Sorry not to have been over before, but we've only just got home. My daughters, Camilla and Josslyn."

More power to Gerda's homely philosophy, Nancy was fervently thinking, if it would but serve as a shield between her and the "frozen folks" she was certain to meet, turning aside the keen blades of their unfriendly criticism, and dulling the sharp edges of their scorn.

Chapter VIII LADY THWAITE's home was an immense Jacobean mansion, more classic but less picturesque in type than gabled, ivy-mantled Bryndham Court. The atmosphere of Athelstane Manor was cheerful, but even to Nancy's inexperienced eyes there was evidence, both in the gray stone house and its spreading parklands, of the stress of hard times from which the estate had suffered. 

Riding home in the limousine, after the tennis game, she was thinking of the interesting little group with whom the afternoon had been spent. With Peter 

Nairne, the very good-looking young Parliamentarian, she had won a hard-fought game from his brother, Derik, and Josslyn. At first it had seemed that they would lose, for Peter had played in a half-hearted way; but his technique had suddenly become brilliant when 

Camilla, with the Honorable Everett Northway Halverley, had come down to the little thatched-roofed audience pavilion. 

Peter Nairne was desperately in love with his cousin, Camilla. That was the first of Nancy's observations.

Later, when they had all gone back to the house for tea, she had noticed that the fervent gaze of the Honorable 

Everett was continually following Lady Thwaite's elder

FLIGHT'S END 

109 daughter, even when he was entertaining herself with the story of some famous astronomer's discovery of a new galaxy of stars.

He was an oldish young man, with thinning brown hair and the gentlest of manners. Once, when Peter and Camilla had stood for a moment together, his dark, handsome head bent above her silvery fairness, there had been such wistful anxiety in Everett Halverley's mild, spectacled eyes that Nancy had felt a throb of genuine pity for him. She had also seen, just then, a revealing softness on Camilla's face; but the girl had turned abruptly away from Peter and crossed the room to sit beside the overwhelmingly grateful Everett.

Alys St. George, the portrait-painter, a fresh-complexioned young woman with snowy hair, had invited Nancy to her next studio tea, and the losers of the tennis game had challenged the winners to another contest, to take place the following week. It had all been very pleasant, but more than once Nancy had felt a disturbing sense of guilt, realizing that her chief interest, for the friends of the late earl, lay in her supposed connection with his recently discovered grandson and heir.

Nancy's eyes widened excitedly. Gerda had given her a new and a very welcome suggestion. Ever since she had discovered the identity of the tall, blue-eyed young man at Flight's End, she had been searching for some pretext that would warrant another ride in that direction. It would be such fun to meet him again, to play up to the mistake he had allowed her to make, pretend she still believed him to be Radcliffe Deane, and all the time thrillingly aware that he was the famous aviator himself. The prospect had a sparkle of adventure which was blithely alluring. And now her ladyship had unconsciously indicated a way in which it might be accomplished.

The week in London was an enchanted one for Nancy. The firm of Maudsley and Carstairs, alternately deserting the law to act as guides for Lord Bryndham and his party, saw more of their city than ever before in their lives, and even so far forgot their dignity as to ride with them in one of the tourist charà-bancs which they had always heartily despised. 

Mr. Carstairs returned with them at the end of the week, prepared to start with Jim the following day on a motor trip to the north of Scotland. It was ostensibly a business journey, to inspect the holdings of the Bryndham estate, but fishing-rods had been packed and maps of Scottish streams had been scanned. 

On the morning of their departure Gerda was waiting in the hall to see them off when Nancy came down the stairs, wearing the new riding-suit, the little hat and the shiny black boots.

It was a glorious Spring day. After a night of rain the earth was clean-washed, and the varying shades of green along the Old Coach Road were a delight to the eye. But Nancy was less observant than usual. Now that she was committed to this enterprise, she was not so sure of the brilliance of her idea. She was half inclined to go back to the village and leave the basket at Mrs. Hawkins' door, instead of carrying out a plan which somehow no longer seemed quite so joyously adventurous and intriguing. But she did not turn. She did not even stop at Crossroads Cottage, but rode on with the reflection that she would see Mrs. Kell on her way home. 

At the corner opposite the suicide's cross she saw an ancient sign, reading: "Private Way." It had toppled to a drunken angle and faced the sky, but its weatherbeaten warning reminded her rather uncomfortably that she was again intruding upon the occupants of Flight's End. Gerda was dusting her boudoir when Nancy went in search of her, to report the appreciation of the “sick lady." It was a task which her ladyship jealously guarded, for it was the only one she was privileged to perform, in the great house with fifty rooms. She did

Nancy never saw those familiar but absurdly inharmonious objects without a fleeting sense of apology to the beautiful, dignified room; but today she was thinking of Gerda, realizing that not once since they came to Bryndham Court had she heard her sing. Chapter IX 

NANCY stopped the roadster between the posts of the gateway at Flight's End, and looked about in search of a tall young man in blue overalls and jumper. She had been wishing that he would be somewhere in sight, to make their arrival seem casual, for she did not relish the idea of being received by the curiously staring nurse. 

The occasion was wholly lacking in the blithe insouciance of her previous visits. She did not at all approve of Gerda's scheme and had fervently hoped for a change of mind, over night. Instead she had found it difficult to restrain her ladyship's benevolent and sentimental urge until a suitable hour of the afternoon. 

There had been one moment when Gerda had faltered. That was when she realized the necessity of using an assumed name. But she had rallied quickly with the brilliant idea of using her maiden name, "Nillson," to which she had as much right as Nancy had to "Deborah." As for her first name, it was to remain "Gerda," for she could never remember to answer to any other.

She was now leaning forward in the car, stretching her neck to peer up at the letters above the arch and spelling out the name which they formed.

In one corner was a grand piano whose top was littered with blue-prints, ash-trays and pipes. Books, papers and magazines were piled high on tables and overflowed to furniture and floor.

A narrow path ran across the garden, under the trellis of an old gate with a wooden seat on one side, and plunged down through an apple orchard whose trees were like round bouquets of creamy-pink blossoms.

Beyond the last of the trees ran the same busy little river that sparkled through the grounds of Bryndham

Court. It was like a silver thread binding the two estates and winding on, through quaint hamlets and wooded parks, to its outlet in the Thames. In the center of it, at this point, was a tiny island containing one magnificent willow tree whose fronds drooped romantically over the stream. About the foot of the tree was a carpet of forget-me-nots, like a bit of blue sky reflected in the crystal water. On the opposite bank, mingled in a gorgeous sworl of color, were marshmarigolds, buttercups and violets, with the lilac-tinted swords of water-mint thrusting up among them. Lancelot's manner changed in a marked degree as they entered his sanctum. The moment before he had been a laughing, bantering boy. Now he was a serious man, a look of utter concentration in his eyes as he talked on a subject of which he was master. He explained the function of the various levers and buttons of the plane and of its many instruments, the navigation sight, to determine drift and speed above the sea; the radio direction finder, by which a wireless station could guide a pilot to his destination; the gyroscopic compass which

FLIGHT'S END 

the wildest buffettings would not turn from its course. 

His enforced period of inaction had not been wasted, for while he was recovering from his own injuries he had studied out a way to overcome the fault that had nearly resulted in disaster. The plane they had been flying, at that time, was his own, and when it was reconstructed, with his new idea incorporated in its mechanism, the invention would be placed at the service of the national air force. 

Never had Nancy known so exciting an experience as this, when the young aviator whose career and attainments the world was watching told her, in his simple, quiet way, of his work and his prediction for the future of aviation. What he foretold was not mere annihilation of time and space, but the knitting together of all the nations of the earth in a brotherhood which in no other way could be realized. 

The sun was casting level rays through the open door of the workshop when they heard the sound of a faint halloo from the house. "That's Gerda's call," said Nancy.

Was she learning to love Lancelot Bryndham? Was that why she thought of him continually, had invented excuses to go to Cotesthorpe, was now so eagerly looking forward to seeing him often while Gerda was making her beneficent visits to the invalid? Was she beginning to care for this young English aviator who was so absorbed in his work, so "wedded to his wings" that he might be as little attracted to her as to the other girls he had known? And if so, what then? In occasional moments of prescience she had sensed that when she loved it would be with all the strength of her nature; but she had never imagined that such love as that would not meet a swift and sure response. Suppose, she challenged herself, she were to give it where it was not wanted, where it would not be returned? 

What a thwarted, unfulfilled thing life would bethen! 

Gerda exclaimed sharply as the runabout swerved toward one of the hedgerows. "Look out, Debby!" she warned. "Are you dreaming, child? Vatch a little vere you're going! 

Nancy gave an odd laugh as she brought the car back to the center of the road. Gerda's admonition had been like an answer to those disturbing thoughts. She had been dreaming; no doubt of that. Perhaps it would be well for her to "watch a little " where she was going.

Chapter X 

ONE of her ladyship's friends was visiting at a country place a few miles from Bryndham Court. Unfortunately the friend had had an accident and was unable to come to the manor-house, so Lady Bryndham would be taken by her niece, each day, to spend several hours at the bedside of the injured one. 

This was the story which had been told to Mrs. Sheppard to account for their frequent absence, and did not, as Gerda righteously affirmed, contain a single untruth. 

With this salve to her conscience, and a perfect orgy of housekeeping in prospect, it was natural that she should be in high spirits as they approached Flight's 

End, the following morning. "Dey are going to have somebody in London find dem a cook," she told Nancy, "but ay hope she don't come wery soon. Believe me, if ay could take care of dat boy for a couple veeks he vould be better. And you could help, too, Debby. Ay vould feed him, you vould cheer him up, and before he knows it he vould be vell again." "I think you'll have to do the cheering-up as well as the feeding," Nancy said quietly. "I'll take you over and I'll call for you, but I don't intend to stay there while you do your work."

FLIGHT'S END 

145 "Ack, Debby!" Gerda remonstrated. "Dat's yost vot dat poor faller needs, to have somebody like you around of him. And Lance vould be glad, too. A blind man vould know he vos tickled to see you yesterday, de vay he smiles ven he looks at you!"

The smile was again convincingly in evidence, when he dashed out to welcome them. "You did come!" he said jubilantly. "I was afraid you'd change your mind." "Not me!" Gerda assured him. "Vid bombs and machine guns you couldn't keep me avay! "

Nancy had not stopped the engine. She leaned to close the door, when Gerda had stepped out.

FLIGHT'S END 

river I was going to take you down to the 'pill.' It's pretty down there." "What's a 'pill' ?" "It's a deep dark pool where the trout hold conventions. We might catch some for lunch." 

Nancy wavered. Since she had, with the fearless honesty of youth, analyzed her feeling for Lancelot 

Bryndham, she had decided to take the advice Gerda had unwittingly given her. She would "watch where she was going." There was no sense, she had sternly told herself, in deliberately placing an inhibition on one's life. She was beginning to care too much for the young man whose heart, as the housekeeper had said, was "in the clouds." For her own peace of mind she must stop thinking about him, and see him as seldom as possible while Gerda was at Flight's End. 

But she had reckoned without the impelling force of Lancelot's eager voice and pleading eyes, and without her own inconsistent longing to make the most of every one of these stray bits of happiness.

Bryndham. She would have that to remember, always.

Gaily they rummaged for poles in the round "bothie " where gardeners' tools had once been kept, and set out for the deep dark "pill" beyond the orchard. The days which followed fairly flew, particularly for Gerda, who was thoroughly enjoying her reign in the big, cheerful kitchen. To her it was nothing short of a dispensation of Providence that no word of the new cook had been received from the London agency to

FLIGHT'S END 

which Lancelot had applied. Evidently it was difficult to secure one who was willing to forego the attractions of city life. Meanwhile Gerda triumphed, for Cliff's appetite was improving, the scales showed a slight increase in his weight and he was noticeably in better spirits. 

Before the end of the third day, in the intimacy of their little "house party," the young people were calling each other by their first names, Cliff using Gerda's "jolly little pet-name " for Nancy; Lancelot preferring the more stately "Deborah," which, as he pronounced it, was a noble and a gracious name.

Sometimes Gerda insisted upon being taken over to Cotesthorpe early in the day, so that she might have sufficient time for bread-making and other activities. 

More often they arrived an hour before noon, when she immediately busied herself with the preparation of a tempting lunch for her "boys."

Lancelot seemed always to know when they were to be expected and was waiting to take Nancy, without ceremony, to the workshop to show her the latest progress on the model plane, or to their favorite bench beside the river, where they would talk, forgetful of time, until Gerda's hail recalled them.

Nancy never ceased to marvel at the close friendship which existed between Lancelot and Cliff, when she came to know their widely differing natures. Cliff was revolutionary; Lancelot was law-abiding. Cliff was a disciple of the modern doctrine of disbelief in all things unseen and unproven, while Lancelot was essentially

FLIGHT'S END 

153 religious. Once, rather shyly, he had confided to her the thoughts that had come to him while he was flying alone, high above the clouds, - thoughts which the mind of man could hardly compass: a vivid realization of the stupendous scope of the universal plan and an overwhelming apprehension of the existence of a Power beyond the plan, directing and guiding it. 

He had a rigid code of honor and of fair play and almost a chivalric attitude toward women, who should, he firmly maintained, be sheltered and protected from the harsher aspects of life. Sometimes Nancy would challenge his views, and later, to her own surprise, would find herself vigorously defending them against 

Cliff's skepticism.

They had been reading a book, one day, which she had greatly enjoyed but which Cliff criticized in scathing terms. 66 Apart from light entertainment, what is there to it?" he demanded. "It reflects none of the great fundamental problems, the bone and sinew and raw meat of life. They are carefully veiled from the reader by a rosy mist of sentiment and false optimism." "Perhaps that's why I like it!" Nancy retorted. "I'm getting awfully tired of raw meat! " "You want the truth, don't you?" "Yes, but the truth isn't always horrid. Your 'raw meat' literature dissects life, shows up all the ugliness of it and ignores the beauty. But there is beauty, so why leave it out of the picture?" "What ho!" jeered Cliff. FLIGHT'S END 

some of Lance's notions! He's the best kind of a good egg, you know, but he really belongs to the Age of Chivalry." "He seems to have done quite a bit for his own age!" she said spiritedly. "Oh rather! Just the same his ideas are medieval!

He thinks chivalry is the antidote for divorce, reverences all women (though he's known precious few and never been in love with any of 'em!) believes in the ultimate triumph of right over wrong, and a lot of other balderdash that's as extinct as the dodo!" "No, Cliff!" Nancy argued. "The old ideals are not extinct. People are beginning to turn back to them. 

They're about ready to give honor and fair play another trial and see if it doesn't result in a better deal for them. They've experimented with all the new ideas and are completely disillusioned. And I'll tell you a secret! Women aren't nearly so keen as they were about their independence. They've enjoyed being man's equal, standing shoulder to shoulder with him; but now they're wondering if the world wouldn't be a better place for them if there were more of the chivalric spirit that you call 'balderdash '!"

His eyes followed her as she left the room. She fancied she could still feel their burning gaze upon her when she stood for a moment outside the door, trying to collect her troubled thoughts before going in search of Gerda. 

Uppermost in her mind was anxiety for Cliff, fear for the effect upon him of definite discouragement at this stage, bewildered uncertainty as to her own course. 

But beneath all was the disturbing realization that the thread of enchantment, which had bound the little group in lighthearted companionship, was broken. The joyous idyl of Flight's End was over.

Chapter XI 

NANCY was strolling, with Josslyn Thwaite, across the wide lawn which stretched between Alys St. George's studio-home and her garden. It was a perfect day and, to the satisfaction of Josslyn, tea was to be served outof-doors. "So much better than being cooped up all afternoon in stuffy rooms," she approved, as she audibly inhaled a breath of fresh air. "I loathe indoor affairs in summer, don't you?" "Yes, I do," Nancy agreed. "Thought you would, because you play such a topping game of tennis. Glad we're to have another go at it tomorrow." 

The younger girl's eyes were fixed admiringly upon 

Nancy. Josslyn's voice was calling to them from the edge of the garden, commanding them to come and have their tea before it was stone cold. There was another hour to be lived through, somehow, before Nancy could get away. Feeling as though she were walking in a dream she went back with Chubby, mingled with the other guests, compelled herself to talk and to laugh and all the time was conscious of but one figure among them, that of Camilla, cool, languid, provocative in the frock which matched her ice-green eyes; Camilla, who was Lancelot's fiancée.

No matter what happened, Jim and Gerda must know about that new will. And it was certain, if Gerda had her way, that Jim would "grow wings "; that the estate would be restored to its rightful owner. Then the "arranged" marriage would take place. Then 

Camilla would become Lancelot's wife.

This was the thought which throbbed agonizingly in

FLIGHT'S END 

Nancy's mind through half the hours of the night. She had flung herself into a deep chair in her boudoir, on her return from the studio-tea, and there she crouched, her cheek pressed close against the faded tapestry, her orchid dress crumpled about her like a wilted flower.

She pictured Camilla in her bridal gown, silvery fair and beautiful; Lancelot slim and tall beside her, smiling down at her. Cliff said Lancelot had never been in love, but he had a chivalric reverence for all women, and perhaps was so fond of Camilla that he was well content with the "arranged" marriage. 

Nancy moaned and buried her face deeper in the curving hollow of the chair. She knew, now, that she had not been honest with herself. She had pretended that the joyous camaraderie with Lancelot was all that she expected; that she was simply storing up memories for a future in which he would have no part. Now she knew that from the start she had believed he might learn to care for her; had of late imagined there was a deeper tone in his voice when he spoke her name, a different light in his eyes when they rested upon her. 

The aching of her heart was almost intolerable when she faced the truth, saw how little their days together had meant to him. They had been merely an interlude between two phases of his busy life. After his first annoyance at her intrusion he had rather welcomed her advent, as a break in the monotony of his self-imposed exile. The comings and goings of an amusingly unconventional American girl had been nothing more than a

FLIGHT'S END 

169 diversion to the fiancé of Camilla Thwaite. 

When she wakened, after a few hours of troubled sleep, she remembered with thankfulness that she need not go to Flight's End that day. Gerda had packed a bag and had taken it with her, the morning before, so that Nancy need not be inconvenienced by calling for her after the tea. She had also announced her intention of staying on, for the balance of the week, as she needed plenty of time for the laundering of some curtains which were too fragile to be entrusted to the hands of the farmer's wife.

But responsibilities were not to be evaded. The mail contained a letter addressed to Lady Bryndham, in Jim's sprawling hand. Nancy knew that it might contain news of his immediate return, and should be taken to Gerda without delay. The housekeeper, who had asked where the letter might be forwarded, was informed that Miss Westcott would herself attend to its delivery.

For the first time she followed the road to Flight's End without the exciting sense of going forth to meet some gay adventure. All Nature seemed to share her mood. A gray mist veiled the contour of the hills, folding them in a brooding silence. The chattering of the birds was hushed, the little river flowed more quietly between its banks and Jet's hoof-beats had a strangely muffled sound. 

But the warmth of Lancelot's greeting was unchanged. He was reading on the porch, and at sight

FLIGHT'S END 

of Nancy gave a shout which was distinctly joyous. He put his hands on her shoulders, when she had dismounted, and gave her a gentle shake. "That's to pay you out for being so jolly mean! "Mrs. Nillson was so sorry for us that she baked all kinds of good things, but nothing could tempt us! "Ha!" Nancy scoffed. "I'll see what she has to say about that." "She'll tell you the same thing. 177 reached the first turning. His head was bowed and he was so deep in thought that he had forgotten to wave. 

Or perhaps he did not care to make his usual friendly gesture to an acknowledged fortune-hunter. 

Nancy's lips were quivering as she rode slowly away.

She had gratified some perverse longing to hit out at him with her bitter words, to taunt him and hurt him, and in so doing had grievously hurt herself. Lancelot had believed in her, in her innate honesty of purpose and in the sincerity of her ideals. That belief, at least, she might have left with him. Now, if he thought of her at all, it would be with contempt for a girl who had forfeited her right to his respect.

Suddenly, to Jet's surprise, she was being turned right-about-face and sent back at a keen gallop over the ground they had just covered. Her rider's eyes were wildly fixed on the turn in the road. Nothing in life was so urgent, at that moment, as to set herself right with Lancelot, assure him that she was not a "gold-digger," confess that she had not meant a word she said,make him believe in her again.

But he was no longer standing beside the gate. He had gone into the house and had closed the door. The stare of the curtainless windows was blank and forbidding. 

It was almost noon. The sun had driven the last shred of mist away and the glad pipings of birds filled the air; but to Nancy, riding disconsolately homeward, the world was a drear, dark thing that day, behind its mask of June.

Chapter XIII LADY THWAITE's dinner, in honor of the Earl of 

Bryndham and his family, was to be their real introduction to their neighbors. Few of the people who had called upon them had found them at home, and when they dutifully returned the formality they had happened to be, as Gerda expressed it, equally "lucky."

To Nancy, on whom the responsibility for Gerda's début rested heavily, the presence of Chubby was a real comfort. Her gown, a floating fantasy of chiffon, the color of daffodils, was vastly becoming and she would have enjoyed the evening if she had not been aware of Gerda's every word and act and continually on the alert to give aid, if need be, to her social inadequacy. She had accepted all of Nancy's teachings with the utmost good-nature and had patiently tried to learn, but habit was tenacious and she occasionally lapsed from her newly acquired standard of etiquette. 

The first tense moment came when Lady Thwaite, having welcomed a new arrival, turned with her toward the honor-guests. With the napkin spread prominently over the façade of Fancine's creation, she was gazing curiously at the people who were her new neighbors, among whom her life was to be spent. 

They carried with them, as perceptible as a perfume, an air of careless, accustomed ease. They were poised and sure, meeting the requirements of the social ritual with the cool casualness of the well-bred. In their cultured voices, with the uneven English cadences and clipped accent which she could not always understand, they were discussing books and art, opera, sports, travel, all topics upon which she was wholly inarticulate. It was the sort of talk which she had often heard in Nancy's home, but she had not then sat at table with the guests. She had been present in the capacity of waitress, wearing cap and apron for the sake of formality, and frequently forgetting to be formal as she urged upon the diners some triumph of her own culinary skill.

She had lingered, talking with Peter Nairne, until the recovery of both pumps had been accomplished, and had joined the others in time to tell them of Lady Bryndham's latest gaucherie.

Gerda plumped herself down upon it. Her feet,

FLIGHT'S END 

forced back into the tight shoes, were causing her intense discomfort, but worse than any physical suffering was the thought of Jim's anger, the remembrance of the look on his face as he stood, aloof and disdainful at the head of the table, talking with Mr. Garland and ignoring her dilemma. That look had cut through her bewilderment and uncertainty and had revealed the truth. She knew, now, what all these people had known from the beginning, that she was a hindrance to Jim. 

She, whose chief aim in life had long been to minister to him, was standing in the way of his success. He was trying to rise to the place which was his by right of birth, and she was holding him back. 

The men had come into the drawingroom and Lady Thwaite was organizing a foursome at one of the card tables. Soon Jim and Mrs. Garland, Mrs. Coxe-Reed and Mr. Garland were deep in a game, the young people were dancing to the music of the wireless in an adjoining reception room, and Sir Nairne and Lady Thwaite were doing their kindly best to entertain Gerda. 

Sitting between them on the davenport, responding vaguely to their efforts, she was enveloped in a curious immobility. She was watching Jim, who was teaching the others a new system of counting that he had learned on the boat. Mrs. Coxe-Reed was leaning close, to read the figures as he wrote them. The smooth plume of her hair almost touched the heavy lock which hung over Jim's forehead. Her skin was the color of the ivory figurines in a glass cabinet beside her. She was smiling

FLIGHT'S END 

209 at Jim with those steel-gray eyes whose glances at Gerda, all through the dinner, had been like tiny blades, pricking, stabbing. And Jim was smiling back at her, admiring, interested and flattered.

The evening came to an end, at last, but not without one more mishap on the part of Gerda. Stumping out of the drawingroom on her aching feet, she blundered against the tall glass cabinet, rattling the precious bits of sculptured ivory and toppling them over on their shelves. In her embarrassment she then started away without taking leave of her hostess, and had to be reminded by Jim of that final requirement. She felt numb, both in body and mind, when they were in the car and on their way home. 

Jim had settled moodily into a corner of the seat and spoke not a word to either of them. Nancy, sitting beside Gerda, took her hand and held it in a warm, consoling clasp. When they reached Bryndham Court she went with her to her boudoir, and lingered sympathetically at the door. "Do you want me to come in for a while, dear?" she asked. "Not tonight, Debby! Ay am too tired to talk.” "Then go straight to sleep and don't worry about anything." 

"All right," Gerda promised dully.

FLIGHT'S END 

London, an anniversary card, on which a gay little

She held the card for a moment against her cheek, her eyes closed, her body swaying from side to side. 

Then she tore it slowly across, again and again, and dropped the pieces into the basket beside the desk.

Chapter XIV 

THE day which followed Lady Thwaite's dinner was a gloomy one. Clouds swept low over the Cotswold hills and a thin rain fell persistently, but about the middle of the morning Gerda came to Nancy's room with an urgent request to be taken over to Flight's End. 

She had not spoken of her catastrophic début, and when Nancy, anxious to ease the wound which lay beneath her unnatural silence, had made light of the whole affair, Gerda had checked her quickly.

It's no use, Debby. Last night ay saw myself like ay look to Yim and to all dose folks, and ay know now vere ay stand." "6 'Jim needn't be so critical!" Nancy said with a flare of indignation. Nancy would have welcomed any excuse further to postpone the visit. She longed and yet dreaded to see 

Lancelot again, for there had been time for misgivings to arise in her mind. She might have imagined she had seen that new radiance in his eyes and heard that vibrant tone in his voice. A man who had been showered with letters from adoring feminine fans would not be particularly thrilled to find that another girl had joined their sentimental ranks. Nancy winced at the thought. At least, she vowed, she would correct that impression at their next meeting. She would be so indifferent, so casual that he would doubt his memory of their moonlit moment beside the little river.

The door at Flight's End was opened to them by the new housekeeper-cook, a slat-like middle-aged woman with wispy gray hair. Lieutenant Bryndham, she said had just left in his plane, to be gone three or four days.

Mr. Deane was in the sittingroom, if the ladies wished to see him.

Nancy told herself that she was greatly relieved at not being obliged to see Lancelot, but the light of expectation faded from her face as she followed Gerda into the familiar room. Chapter XV 

GERDA was sitting near a window of her boudoir, darning Jim's socks while Nancy read aloud. It was a particularly gripping tale, chosen for the express purpose of diverting her thoughts from gloomy channels, but frequent gusty sighs, irrelevant to the action of the story, proved that her attention was wandering. 

Her first active interest in life, that morning, was displayed when Duff presently came to announce that his lordship was on the telephone, calling from London. 

Gerda heaved herself vigorously from her chair. 

Her cheeks were suddenly bright with color. "Run along, child," she beamed, "and tell him ay am coming!" "Yes, m'lady, but-it was Miss Westcott that his lordship particularly asked to speak to."

Gerda halted, half way across the room, staring at the girl. "He ain't asked to speak to me?" she questioned bleakly. "I-I'm sure, m'lady," Duff hesitated, "that it was your niece he asked for." 

Nancy put down her book. "Business before pleasure, with Uncle Jim! Better come along and say "Hello!""

FLIGHT'S END 

223 Gerda shook her head and went slowly back to her chair. (6 

Shall I tell him anything for you?" asked Nancy, at the door. "No," Gerda said tonelessly. "Tell him not'ing for me."

It was in a resentful frame of mind that Nancy went down to answer Jim's call. He had gone to the city on an early train that morning, to attend to some estate matters, and had left without saying goodbye to Gerda.

He had not spoken to her at all, since the evening at Lady Thwaite's, except to remind her of the dinner they were giving during the Garlands' week-end visit to 

Bryndham Court.

His way of showing his displeasure was to retire into a shell of silence, and instead of helping matters it was simply making Gerda acutely miserable. He should be able to recognize the fact that she was doing her best, but that she could not be made over in a few weeks. 

His own ways and manners were still too unpolished, Nancy thought wrathfully, to warrant such severe criticism of his wife.

Her voice, when she spoke into the transmitter, was so glacial that Jim did not recognize it. "That you, Nancy?" he asked. "Yes." "O. K. There's something I want you to do for me.

She crinkled her nose at the instrument as she put it back in its anachronistic hiding-place behind the armored "skeleton." She was remembering that this date, June the 12th, was Jim and Gerda's anniversary, an occasion which had always been celebrated with much festivity in their little home. That was why 

Gerda had flushed so brightly, anticipating a reconciliatory message from her husband in honor of the day. 

It was the first year since their marriage that she had not made a glistening white" wedding cake," and surmounted it with a tiny wax bride and groom. The wee dolls had been brought out, within the last week, and garbed in fresh bridal attire, but the day following Lady Thwaite's dinner Nancy had seen Gerda, her face poignant, replacing them in their tissue-paper wrappings. 

As she gave the order for the car and went back to her

FLIGHT'S END 

225 reading, Nancy was meditating certain bits of advice which might be salutary, if she could summon courage to offer them to Jim. But he was of an inordinately secretive temperament, and would no more willingly accept her interference in his domestic affairs than the "muddling" of Miss Wicks through his personal belongings. 

Fortunately the secretary was having her lunch when the messenger from Mr. Maudsley's office arrived. 

Grimsby was looking affronted, when Nancy came down, for the young man had refused to deliver the key of Lord Bryndham's desk into any other hands than those of his lordship's niece. The butler's nose was still haughtily atilt as he led the clerk down the hall to the morning-room, where his lunch was to be served. 

Jim had fitted a new top to the large roll-top desk which the old earl had used. It was always closed when he was out of the study, and Nancy had not seen the small labels which he had recently placed on all the pigeonhole drawers. There were two sets of them, one on either side of the desk, each bearing, in Jim's writing, the name of its contents. She began with the group at the left, where she had once seen him deposit some personal papers, and read the labels: "Income 

Taxes," "Receipts," "Maps," and in the far corner a drawer marked: "Private - Keep Out." 

Nancy smiled as she read that curt command. The same prohibitive words had been painted on the door of Jim's tool-closet, in their bungalow. Nobody but

FLIGHT'S END 

Gerda had been likely to enter it, even if he had not always kept it locked, but the sign had been carefully lettered on one of the panels and doubtless satisfied that intensely individualistic strain in his nature. 

The drawer contained only a package of old lottery tickets, a list of horses that had run in some race and a long envelop inscribed "STRICTLY PERSONAL." 

The envelop was sealed, but Nancy managed to open it without tearing the gummed flap. She took out several folded sheets and glanced through them quickly. 

There were some pages from an account book, a long paper, yellow with age and covered with writing, and a clipping from a Los Angeles newspaper. She recognized the clipping as the one which Jim had happened to see as he unwrapped the papers from an automobile tire. It read: "Will anyone having knowledge of the whereabouts of James Lancelot Bryndham, who left England for the United States in 1876, or any of his descendants, communicate at once with Messrs. Maudsley and Carstairs, 18, Charles Street, St. James Square, London, England." 

What a lucky chance for Jim, Nancy thought, that he had chanced upon that bit of printing, in a newssheet which he seldom saw, with its vital message for himself.

She replaced the clipping in the envelop and began to look more carefully at its other contents. This time she noticed that there were three names at the foot of

FLIGHT'S END 

227 the long, closely-written sheet, a signature and two bracketed names with a curiously spelled word, “Witnessers,” beneath them. Could that, she wondered, be the birth certificate? The writing was in an unformed hand, almost like that of a child. She ran her eyes over the first few lines: "This is a writeing for you my boy Jimmie that you Aunt Teresa giv you when you are beeg. Jimmie doctor say I die soon so I write somthing you must kno. Jimmie you father no was James Bryndham like I always tell you. James Bryndham's baby die and I give you the ded baby's name but you really father was Grant Curtin son of boss of beeg mine, I meet him when I come here with my bruther Francisco and his wife Teresa, and get jobb to help in bar -— 

Nancy gave a sharp cry and dropped the yellowed sheet to the desk. Jim not the son of James Bryndham?

But then what right had he to the name, to the title the inheritance? If he had claimed them, knowing he had no right to them - he was an impostor. 

"Oh, no, no!" Nancy wailed. "I can't believe it! 

Jim wouldn't do that! "

She snatched up the paper and glanced at the bottom of the written lines. It was signed: "Thy mother, Consuelo," and witnessed by Francisco and Teresa Rubio. 

Panic-stricken, Nancy ran her hands through her hair, got up and walked agitatedly about the study.

FLIGHT'S END 

"What shall I do? What shall I do?" she frantically asked herself. "If this is true, if Jim has done this thing, it'll simply break Gerda's heart. But if he is guilty, pretending to be the grandson of the Earl of

She glanced at her watch. She would have to put the incriminating paper back in its place, find the birth certificate, lock the desk and send the key back to Jim. The thin, yellowed sheet shook in her hands. There it was, proof beyond the shadow of a doubt that Jim was an impostor. Knowing himself to be the illegitimate son of an American gambler and a Mexican bar-room dancer, he had answered the Earl of Bryndham's advertisement; had claimed the inheritance on the strength of the "birth-paper" of James Lancelot 

Bryndham's baby, the baby who had died. 

The characteristics of his "really " father had evidently been reproduced in Jim. He, too, had a flair for gambling and loved games of chance. This time he had played for high stakes. Whether or not he had ever used the blackmailing weapon which his mother had placed in his hands, he evidently considered it of value for the hold it gave him over the "rich proud familly" of Grant Curtin, and for that reason had retained it, carefully guarded always by locks and warning signs.

Dazedly Nancy folded the paper, restored it to the envelope and drawer, and set about the business of finding the birth certificate. Among the pigeonholes on the right-hand side of the desk was one marked "Personal." In it she found the short, hand-written document which recorded the birth of a son to James Lancelot Bryndham and his wife, Consuelo. If she had listened more carefully to Jim's instructions, had opened the drawer labelled "Personal" instead of the one with the injunction: "Private - Keep Out," his secret would never have been discovered.

She closed the desk and, taking the certificate, went

FLIGHT'S END 

231 out of the study. Half way across the library she stopped short, struck by a devastating thought. If she exposed Jim's trickery, forced him to restore the inheritance to its rightful owner, it would revert to the next of kin, and the next of kin was Lancelot. And that, she suddenly saw with blinding clearness, would be giving Lancelot back-to Camilla.

Nancy dropped down on the arm of the nearest chair and pressed her hand hard against her eyes, as if to shut out that direful vision. Passionately she was wishing she had not found the paper, and that everything could go on as before. It was almost certain that 

Camilla would marry Everett Halverley. Then Lancelot would be free. That was what his glowing look had meant. That was what he was hoping to tell her, soon, that he had been released from the "arranged" engagement which in honor bound him, was free to marry the girl he loved.

A sob caught in Nancy's throat. After having seen love in Lancelot's eyes, heard it in the tones of his voice, how could she give him up? How could she bear to think of her life going on without him? Her chance for happiness was within her grasp. Why should she not take it? 

And there was Gerda to be considered, - Gerda, whose love for Jim was based upon implicit faith in his honesty and goodness. Worse than death, for 

Gerda, would be the awakening to a knowledge of Jim's real nature. Even though Lancelot were lenient,

FLIGHT'S END 

even though Jim should not be made to pay the penalty for his crime, Gerda's heart would be broken. Was it not, Nancy fiercely demanded of herself, her duty to hold her peace, conserve Gerda's happiness, her own -and Lancelot's?

Almost before the question had taken shape in her mind the answer was presenting itself. Her generation was supposed to be ruthless, to take what it wanted regardless of consequences, but within her was something which held her grimly to the course that she knew was right. It was unthinkable that she should become a party to Jim's fraud, that she should deprive Lancelot of his inheritance, close the doors of his home, and all that it implied, against him. 

She raised her head and looked about her, at the beautiful room, its books, its rare treasures. It symbolized for her the spirit of the house, its memories and its legends. She knew how dearly Lancelot, with his sensitiveness to beauty and his deep reverence for tradition, must love the old place. It was his to hold and to preserve, his to pass on to future generations. 

Not Jim's, to be won on a dishonest gamble with luck and perhaps, since the estate was not entailed, gambled away later on for some other big stake. Ancestral home and fortune were Lancelot's by law and by right. 

Her duty was plain. Neither her own happiness nor 

Gerda's must influence her action.

But what if Jim, when accused, should deny everything? Of what use was her discovery without proof?

FLIGHT'S END 

233 Jim would simply destroy the paper, defy her, dare her to do her worst. If only she had time to copy it. 

Copy it? Why a copy? Nothing but the original could serve her purpose. Nancy gasped as the thought surged through her mind. She shrank from it even while she realized that in no other way could Jim be compelled to make restitution. With that evidence in her hands, Lancelot's inheritance would be safe.

She got up and ran back to the study. Quickly, before her resolution had time to weaken, before her courage had time to falter, she re-opened the desk, pulled out the little drawer, took the long, close-written sheet from the envelope. The desk-top rolled down with a dull, heavy rumble. It sounded in Nancy's ears like the clanging of a prison door upon her hopes and her dreams.

The messenger from Mr. Maudsley's office, having finished lunch, was wandering about the hall looking in an impressed way at the suit of armor, the swords and the shields. He started when Nancy came quietly up to him. For a moment he thought he was seeing the ghost of one of those dames whose portraits hung against the walls, for she was as white as the dress she was wearing and there was a strange, unseeing gaze in her wide eyes. She smiled when she gave him the paper Lord Bryndham had sent for and the key to his lordship's desk; but the young man could not quite rid himself of that first gruesome impression. It haunted him all the way back to London, on the 1:50 train.

Chapter XVI 

WITHIN an hour after Jim's return to Bryndham 

Court, the following day, one of the maids came to

Believing he had already discovered his loss, and quaking at the prospect of the coming scene, Nancy slowly descended the stairs. It was not the Jim 

Bryndham she had always known who was awaiting her; not the quiet, law-abiding person who had been

It was a man of different type, one who was capable of conceiving and carrying out a crime, - and Nancy had a wholesome fear of criminals. She stood for a moment at the door of the study, bracing her courage for the encounter.

Jim was seated at his desk, and to her astonishment smiled amiably as he looked up at her. (6

The "feed" proved to be all that the most exacting of hosts could require, Mrs. Sheppard having surpassed herself in her endeavor to restore the old-time brilliance and dignity of such functions at Bryndham Court. And on this occasion Gerda was guilty of no "breaks." She ate little, talked not at all except when spoken to and then replied in so low a tone that only those nearest her could hear. 

Nancy had been careful to place Mrs. Coxe-Reed at a discreet distance from Gerda. She was seated near the head of the table and within conversational reach of Jim. With a slight flush on his dark face he leaned to talk with her, seemed vastly entertained by her conversation and laughed immoderately at her scintillating wit. Later, while the other guests played cards or danced and Gerda sat silently watching, he and Mrs. 

Coxe-Reed bent together over a map of South America, where she had mining interests, and Jim, stimulated by her flattering attention, told story after story of his early experiences in the camps of Dakota.

To Nancy the whole affair had a peculiar unreality. 

It was like watching a play, in which the actors spoke their lines and made their gestures, while she alone, of them all, knew the climax which the last act was to bring. She dreaded the ordeal that lay before her, yet was thankful when the dinner was over and glad when the Garland family's visit, so long and pleasurably anticipated, had come to an end.

Nancy could hardly believe the evidence of her own eyes. She sat stupidly watching him, as if even yet he might burst out with the vicious threats, the ravings, the violence she had expected. After a while she got up and walked across the room, where she could not see that pitiful, trapped look, which was unbearably distressing. It was almost grotesque to think that she had been afraid of such a man. He was no hardened criminal. He was a coward, who had taken a chance in an instant of bravado, and now that he had lost was stricken with terror. She could imagine how, under a gambling impulse, he had answered the Earl of Bryndham's advertisement, and she could also fancy the trepidation with which he had carried on the deception after it was begun. She was remembering that she had several times seen a furtive, frightened gleam in his eyes; first in the hour of their arrival at Bryndham

Court, later when he had looked up at the portrait of the old earl, and again when one of James Bryndham's friends had asked him to tell some of his memories of his supposed father. Jim's swagger had covered not merely diffidence and awkwardness, but an ever-present consciousness of guilt.

He had pulled himself together, when she next looked at him, but was still ghostly pale and his voice, when he spoke, was flat and toneless. "What are you 

going to do?" he asked. 

There's only one thing that I can do, Jim," she

There was not much danger of his attempting to get away. The mere thought of Scotland Yard had been enough to bring that look of stark fear again to his eyes. It was there when Nancy went out and closed the door upon the most harrowing half-hour of her life. 

The old saying was right, she was thinking miserably. 

Money had made for Jim, for Gerda, for all of themmore trouble than happiness.

Chapter XVII 

MR. MAUDSLEY had arrived at Bryndham Court in response to an urgent telephone message from her ladyship's niece. Thin, erect, immaculately groomed, he was even more dapper than usual that afternoon, in gray suit, hat and spats, with a tie which matched the infinitesimal mauve line of the suit and a tiny pink rosebud in the buttonhole of his lapel. His step, as he entered the reception-room in which Nancy awaited him, was almost sprightly, and he was conscious of a certain exhilaration of spirits, by no means unpleasant for seventy, when she came forward to meet him. One was never too old, after all, to feel the charm of youth and beauty.

When she began to talk he sat inclined solicitously toward her, his regard a subtle blending of admiration and courteous attention. But as her narrative progressed both attitude and expression changed. His bushy white brows jutted at a truculent angle, his lips became grim, his monocle took on a startled and outraged gleam. He did not interrupt except, at intervals, with an explosive "Preposterous! He uttered the same word three times in rapid succession, at the close of her incredible story. 

---

FLIGHT'S END 

Chagrin was the old solicitor's first emotion. Never before, in the long and honorable existence of the firm of Maudsley and Carstairs, had so grievous an error been made. It would be a staggering blow to the reputation they had achieved for an almost uncanny insight and cleverness in detecting the faintest evidence of fraud. Despite the care with which they had investigated the American's claim to the title and estate of the 

Earl of Bryndham, from every possible angle, they had been hoodwinked, tricked, led by the nose.

Mr. Maudsley's own eagle beak became faintly roseate at the thought. His indignation increased as he realized more and more clearly the brazen audacity of this illegitimate son of a bar-room dancer and a gambler, who had not only deprived the rightful heir of his inheritance, duped the friends of the late earl, belittled the honorable traditions of the house of Bryndham, but had exposed to criticism—and ridicule — the legal efficiency of Maudsley and Carstairs. 

He puffed out his lips and scowled at the young woman who sat there before him, actually pleading for leniency for the rascal who had outwitted them all. (C "Leniency! " With a snort and a contemptuous fillip of his fingers, Mr. Maudsley flouted the idea. "There is such a thing as justice in this country!" he snapped.

This man has fraudulently represented himself to be the heir of the late Earl of Bryndham, has deprived my client, the next of kin and the real heir, — of his privileges for a period of nearly two months and would 

---

FLIGHT'S END 

249 have done so indefinitely had you not discovered this - ah document which you have submitted to me. Your action in the matter has been most commendable, but you surely do not imagine that such a crime can be pardoned?" 

Nancy's wide, troubled eyes met his earnestly. "Not under ordinary circumstances. But I have been told that your client dislikes publicity, and it seemed to me that it might be better for everybody, if it could be avoided." 

"Ha!" said Mr. Maudsley, and tugged reflectively at one of his jutting brows.

She had unknowingly used a potent argument. It was true that Lancelot Bryndham abhorred publicity, and he was now so important a figure in the public eye that any story concerning him would be featured in every paper in the country. Also, if the affair never came to the notice of the press, the firm of Maudsley and Carstairs would escape endless criticism. "There is one way in which it could be done," Nancy was saying hesitantly. "It was Jim's idea and you may not care to consider it, but I promised I would suggest it to you. He thought a story might be given out to the effect that he had decided to recognize the validity of that memorandum-will and was turning the estate back to - your client."

It was an hour later when the solicitor came out onto the porch at Flight's End and raised a forefinger as a signal to Nancy, who was waiting in the limousine. He was prepared to admit, to all and sundry, that the ways of the younger generation were past finding out. His client, informed of the restitution of the estate which he had supposed lost to him, had received the news with something like consternation. And now this young woman, whom he had considered unusually sensible, was coming up the steps looking as flushed and starry-eyed as any other silly hero-worshipper, at the prospect of meeting the famous aviator.

Chapter XVIII 

THE clock in the library at Bryndham Court was chiming the hour of three when Mr. Maudsley entered, black leather brief-case in hand, and glanced severely about the room. He looked, Nancy thought, like an elderly avenging angel about to wield the sword of retributive justice, and was plainly in no mood for "playacting.' 

Jim had himself set the stage for this scene. Learning that Gerda was at all costs to be kept in ignorance of his wrong-doing, he had arranged for her to be in the library while Mr. Maudsley was there, trusting to her presence to bottle up the solicitor's wrath. "I don't want her to know what it's all about till afterward," he had told Nancy. "She'll get a bigger kick out of it, that way, after seeing me sign the paper. 

You can explain it to her later, when the old wolf has gone." 

Nancy understood. He would use Gerda as a shield, but would spare himself the embarrassment of being lauded for his magnificent deed while Mr. Maudsley's baneful glare was upon him.

He had regained some of his assurance when he heard of Lancelot's decision to avoid the publicity which was

FLIGHT'S END 

so distasteful to him, and learned that his own scheme" to explain the change of tenure at Bryndham Court was to be adopted. But as the time for the solicitor's arrival drew near his nervousness had increased. He was now pacing restlessly on the lawn beneath the library windows, mustering his bravado for the encounter with the "old wolf."

Mr. Maudsley proffered a limp hand to Nancy, when she went forward to greet him. "Where's that fellow?" he asked without preamble. "He's right here. I'll call him."

She hesitated a moment before going to the window, looking appealingly at the old man. "You will be careful, won't you," she asked, "if 

Gerda comes in?”

His answer was a grunt as he placed his brief-case on an ancient Bible-rest and snapped back its fastenings. 

When Nancy turned, after signalling to Jim, she saw that Gerda had slipped into the room and was sitting on a chair beside the door, her hands folded in her lap. 

She did not seem to notice the chill gravity of Mr. 

Maudsley's bow. She sat as if lost in thought, gazing unseeingly into space.

Fierce resentment blazed up in Nancy at sight of that patient figure, toward which the old Englishman's monocle had sent an inimical glint. She wanted to challenge him, so smugly certain of his superiority to the ignorant Swedish wife of "that fellow," to match

FLIGHT'S END 

267 the courage and the heroism with which Gerda was facing martydom.

Quietly and steadily she was making ready to take herself out of Jim's life. Little by little, so that he should not notice their disappearance, she was packing her simple belongings, choosing only those which had been hers before prosperity came. Nancy had tried in vain to soothe her with hints of changes that were to come. Her way was plain before her and she was not to be turned from it. 

Going into her room, that morning, Nancy had seen her standing before Jim's dresser, one of his ties crushed against her lips, making soft little moaning sounds that cut the girl to the heart. "Gerda, dear!" she had pleaded. "Won't you believe me when I tell you this is all unnecessary? Something is going to happen. I can't explain yet, but- 

Gerda shook her head. "Ay must go, Debby," she said, a crucified look on her face. "If ay stay ay vould hang like a rock round Yim's neck, and some day he vould hate me! Do you t'ank ay vould let dat happen? Dat ay see hate in his eyes for me? Gud förbjud!" 

It was cruel to allow her to carry that burden even for an additional hour, but Nancy could not disclose 

Gerda's purpose to Jim. That was her secret. And

Jim had decreed silence until his own purpose had been served.

She had endured, dry-eyed and in despairing silence, the agony of renunciation, but now, when the unbelievable had happened, when faith in Jim and hope for the future had miraculously been restored, her face puckered like a baby's, tears streamed down her cheeks and she broke into violent sobbing. Nancy rose to heights of heroism above herself, at that moment. Her decision was made. She knew, when she said goodbye to him and watched him running across the lawn, waved gaily as he turned at the rustic bridge, that she would not see him again. She would be on her way home, when he came, at the end of "one more day," to Bryndham Court.

Chapter XIX 

CAMILLA THWAITE frowned as for the third time she sounded the knocker on the ancient oak door at Cotesthorpe. The door stood wide open and she could look into the hall where she had often played with Lancelot and his sister, when they were children. The old place had been dilapidated then, but now it was almost in ruins and she could not imagine why Lancelot had chosen to stay in so forlorn a spot. 

She had only just learned his address, for his occasional letters had been forwarded to her from Mr. 

Maudsley's office and had never given the slightest clew to his hiding-place. It had been an absurd performance, this going into exile for several months, shutting himself away from the world to care for his invalid chum, but it was like Lance to do it. He had always, Camilla thought, as she again impatiently rattled the knocker, been ridiculously extravagant in his ideas of loyalty.

Someone was coming, at lasta wispy-haired woman, tying a white apron around her lath-like waist and squinting sleepily at the caller. "Good afternoon, miss," she said respectfully. "Is Lieutenant Bryndham in?" Camilla asked. 66 

No, miss. He went away this morning, in his car."

FLIGHT'S END 

(c Do you know when he will be back?" "No, I don't, miss, but perhaps Mr. Cliff can tell you.' 

May I see him?" "Yes, miss. He's in there. Will you go right in?” "If you'll tell me which room," Camilla said crisply.

The woman came closer, blinking against the light from the open doorway. "Oh, I beg pardon, miss! I thought it was another young lady that's been here to see Lieutenant Bryndham. What name shall I say, please? " "Never mind. I'll announce myself." 

Cliff was reading when she opened the sittingroom door. The involuntary eagerness of his first glance was followed by a blank stare of disappointment, but he rose and came with simulated pleasure to meet her. "Camilla!" he exclaimed. "This is a surprise! "Did you expect to see someone else?" she asked. "No." "Oh, I thought perhaps you did." 

She looked curiously at him as he cleared an ashtray and some books from one of the chairs. She had always considered him an exceptionally attractive man. Now, with his wasted frame and thin, pale face he was like a shadow of his former self. "Did you come over alone?" he was asking. 66 "Yes. I wanted to see Lance."

"Who is the woman Lance is infatuated with?" she asked bluntly, when Cliff came back to his place.

He did not move but Camilla flinched involuntarily as his glance touched her. It was like the flick of a whiplash. "Careful!" he warned, between set lips.

She walked up and down the driveway while he was gone, thinking with growing anger of the indignities that had been heaped upon her. It had been insulting of Lancelot to ask for his release, as if she were a person whose claim might lightly be tossed aside. Then there was his threat of renouncing the inheritance, which was too absurd to be considered seriously. And now Cliff, with his unwarranted impertinence, had added to her humiliation.

In ominous silence she took the coat and cap which he presently brought her, and followed him down through the orchard. She could scarcely bring herself to take his hand, when he assisted her to her place in the forward cockpit of the two-passenger airplane.

The take-off was fairly good, but Camilla had flown often enough with Lance to recognize the difference between his firm, sure piloting and the erratic guidance of 

Cliff's thin hands on the controls. He flew too low, at the start, just skimming the tops of the trees, then went zooming up and down again. She turned and called a remonstrance, but her voice was drowned in the roaring of the motor and Cliff did not heed her anxious gesture.

They were climbing, now, soaring high above hills and hamlets, following the course of the little river which lay across fields of corn and meadows red with poppies like a silver scarf on a garment of gold and

FLIGHT'S END 

289 scarlet and green. But Camilla was unconscious of the beauty of the scene. She was looking ahead, eagerly watching for the great gray roof of Athelstane Manor. 

She was decidedly uneasy, having realized at last that Cliff was still a sick man, and evidently unfit for the strain of flying. 

She jumped nervously when something touched her shoulder. It was a folded paper, which Cliff was leaning to hand to her. She saw, as she wonderingly opened it, that it was a note, written in ink, on a sheet of the same stationery that she had used a few moments before. "I told you, Camilla," she read, "that I would give my life for Lancelot, if need be. 

I meant that. I'm ready to step out of the picture if I can bring happiness to him- and to the girl he loves. But I can only do that by taking you with me. "I'm going to give you one more chance to do the decent thing. If you'll promise to release Lance and never tell anyone your reason for doing so 

- - you'll be safe at home in a few minutes. If not then the end of this flight will be the end of everything both of us." - for She crushed the paper violently in her hands. She was almost choking with rage. This was the last straw.

She had been insulted, cheapened, humiliated, and now they were trying another trick to force her to their will.

FLIGHT'S END 

Give his life for Lancelot? Not he! It was a cheap, melodramatic trick, and he had so poor an opinion of her mentality that he expected her to believe his lie. 

She caught up the note, tore it into tiny bits and tossed them contemptuously to the winds; then twisting about in the seat she stormed her defiance at Cliff. "That's my answer! I'm not to be coerced by your cheap tricks! Now- take-me-home!”

He could not fail to get her meaning, even though he did not hear the words, for her expression and motions were emphatic. Her lips curled in an ironic smile as he gravely nodded. This would teach him to be careful when he picked his next victim for an aerial farce. 

He should have known better than to try such tactics with her.

But her triumph was short-lived. Almost at once she noticed that he was changing the course of the plane. He was turning it to the west, toward the 

Thames, and rising higher and higher. She screamed at him, pointing urgently in the direction of her home. 

His eyes turned briefly upon her and an icy chill ran over her body. Those eyes! Smouldering in a face that was like a livid mask, they struck terror to her heart. It wasn't a trick. He was in earnest. The truth flashed upon her. The man was mad. He had planned this thing. He had done something to her car so that she could not use it. He had written that note while she was waiting for him. He had brought her up there to kill her. 

---

FLIGHT'S END 

291 She flung herself to the edge of the plane, as if hoping that from the peaceful countryside beneath help might come, and recoiled in horror when she saw how far below it lay. She cowered in the corner of the cockpit. In imagination she already felt the frightful downward rush through space. Half paralyzed with fear, she shrieked when he again touched her shoulder.

He was saying something when she peered around at him. She could not hear his voice, but she could read the word his lips formed: “Promise?” 

The spinning universe seemed suddenly to right itself. All she had seen was death, riding with her, so near, so inevitable that she had forgotten there was a way of escape. Promise? She would promise anything if it would save her life. Lancelot, their marriage, the joining of their estates, the money-what did they all amount to, compared with life? They seemed as unimportant as the smallest blade of grass that grew down there, so far below-down there on the earth, where there was safety. 

She nodded vehemently. "Yes, yes, yes!" she clamored above the droning of the motor. "I "I promise! 

I promise! 

His eyes were upon her, burning into hers, questioning, probing for the spirit of her pledge. 

She held up a trembling hand, as if taking an oath. "I promise! I promise! I'll do what you say! I will! I will! I swear I will! "

FLIGHT'S END 

Anything, anything, to be safe again, out of the power of that madman. Anything, anything — for life.

Before the plane had quite stopped bumping over the meadow beyond the tennis courts, at Athelstane

Manor, she was out of the cockpit, falling to her knees as she touched the ground, scrambling to her feet and starting away. Cliff had slumped, in utter exhaustion, in his seat. His face was drawn and haggard but there was a faint smile on his lips as he watched Camilla running, stumbling across the field to her home. He had brought her back in time for tea.

Chapter XX 

GERDA was standing at the rail of an ocean-liner, watching the shifting scenes on the Southampton dock and softly humming her "happy song." Now that she was on her way home, leaving forever behind her the griefs attendant upon rank and revenue, she was lightheartedly enjoying the incidents of travel.

The boat-train on which they had come down from London had discharged its passengers, but others were arriving by bus and by motor, friends were taking leave of each other, porters were wheeling hand-trucks piled high with baggage, an old man with a violin was playing tuneful airs and just below, on the pier, was a group of young people whose shouts of laughter brought wide, responsive smiles to Gerda's lips. "See, Debby!" she said gleefully. "Some newlyveds is coming along on dis boat. Dat girl vid de bunch of flowers is de bride and de tall, nice-looking boy is de husband. He makes me t'ank a little of Lance, dat young faller." 

Nancy murmured assent but turned her eyes away from the “ tall, nice-looking boy " whose flashing smile had reminded her, too, of Lance. She should have told 

Gerda, she was thinking drearily, and spared herself these unbearable reminiscences. Yet it would have

FLIGHT'S END 

been cruel to allow the shadow of her trouble to darken such utter felicity as Gerda's in this moment of her triumph. 

She was going home, not heartsick and bereft as she had expected to go, but with her beloved Jim miraculously restored to her from the clutches of British society and particularly from those of Mrs. Willoughby Coxe-Reed. She was going back to take up again the simple ways that she loved, and the face of the young bride was no more radiant than Gerda's as she looked toward the blissful future. Her body was there, on the deck of the ship, but her spirit was on ahead, — opening the little bungalow, taking the covers off the new davenport, hanging fresh ruffled curtains in the windows and tying up the tendrils of the pretty vine above the porch. 

"You know, Debby," she mused, " you vould maybe t'ank ay am foolish, but it seems like ve vos yost beginning all over, Yim and me! Dis is our second vedding trip," she added with her rich chuckle, " only ay vouldn't change places vid dem kids down dere because ve found out, already, Yim and me, vot ve vant from life."

She rummaged in her hand-bag, found a shilling and dropped it into the hat which the aged violinist was appealingly lifting toward his audience on the deck. "Dere you are, old man," she laughed, “and ay vish you vos as lucky as me! Ay am so glad for everyt'ing," she went on rhapsodically. "Ay am glad ay
