A fleeting smile touched Nancy's lips. Jim's "homesickness" had developed soon after the meeting in the library, for within the hour he had begun to find his heroic role an embarrassment. The secret was already public property. His "confidential" chat with Mrs. 

Coxe-Reed had taken place in front of the shop where petrol was sold, in the village, and had been overheard by the man who was filling the tank of her car. The news had spread. Someone had brought it to the house, where it was confirmed by the presence of Lancelot, by a sentence Lady Thwaite had spoken as she went with him along the hall, and by Gerda's radiant face.

The housekeeper, shaken by emotion from her usual dignity, had shed tears on Jim's hand when she grasped it and tried to express her wonder and admiration. The way the servants watched him, with awed, reverent eyes, was getting on his nerves. The villagers were preparing a demonstration in his honor, and ahead loomed Lady Thwaite's dinner, with Lancelot as one of the guests, to watch with clear, disconcerting eyes while the pretender was eulogized for his "nobility."

FLIGHT'S END 

297 

Nancy had not expected them to hurry their departure; had rather hoped to slip away alone. But when she announced her intention to leave at once

Jim had caught at the idea and had declared that he and Gerda would accompany her. 

Mrs. Sheppard had made an impressive speech when they came away, the next day, punctuated by sniffs from Duff and applause from the balance of the staff. 

They flocked out in a body to the terrace to wave as the limousine started down the avenue of elms. That was the picture which Nancy took away with her as she looked back for the last time at the stately house, at the trees, the fountain and the garden. Like a faint call came the memory of the sundial's exhortation: Time Flies 

Lines Rise and Shadows Fall 

Let it Pass By

Love Reigns Forever Over All 

She had left a letter for Lancelot, to be given to him when he arrived at Bryndham Court that afternoon.

Long before the hour which he had set for his return they were in London and in communication with Mr. 

Maudsley. Over the telephone, when Nancy called him, the solicitor's voice had held an unnatural note of restraint and his replies had been so guarded that she was certain Lancelot was with him at that very moment, setting in motion the wheels of his " quixotic

FLIGHT'S END 

plan." An hour later the old man had come hurrying into their hotel, unflatteringly eager to speed them on their way, to make steamship reservations, to send for their tickets, to arrange for necessary funds. 

He was tactful to a degree, accepting without a word of comment a bank-note which Nancy gave him, to cover the cost of her own passage. His manner toward her, she thought with a touch of whimsy, was like that of a devoted prime minister to a queen who was ap proaching the guillotine. 

He took her hand, at parting, and murmured a few stilted but earnest words. "I want you to know, Miss Westcott," he had said, "how highly I esteem and admire you for your courage and unselfishness, and to assure you that under other-ah-circumstances my attitude would have been quite different.' 

Jim's face had worn a relieved expression as he saw the solicitor make his formal bow and take himself, with precise steps, out of the room and out of their lives. "Funny old bird!" he had grunted. "As English as roast beef and Yorkshire pudding! 

"Vot vos de matter vid de man?" Gerda had puzzled. "In place of saying nice t'ings to you for vot you did, he acts like he is mad!' 

"That," Jim said with an impish glint at Nancy, "is just British reserve. He could say plenty if he let himself go!"

FLIGHT'S END 

299 

Very early the next morning an efficient clerk from

Mr. Maudsley's office had arrived with travelers' checks and with tickets for a steamer which was sailing at noon, and did not leave them until he had seen them safely installed in the compartment that had been reserved for them on the boat-train. 

A change came over Jim with the exit of the clerk.

He was no longer the half-defiant lord of the manor, carrying on a game which taxed his courage, nor the frightened culprit whose trickery had been discovered. 

To Nancy he now seemed like the Jim Bryndham of the old days, quiet and reticent, but with no impelling ambition beyond the success of the garage in which he had a half interest and the driving of Miss Kenton's car. Already he had abandoned the more erect carriage which he had of late acquired, and was settling into his familiar slouching gait. Hands in pockets, his black pipe in mouth, he had gone on an inspection tour of the liner, and was now strolling slowly back to join his wife and Nancy. 

Gerda hailed him with a cheerful hoot. "Hi, Yim!

Nancy turned abruptly and walked along the deck. 

It would not be for her as if they had never been away. 

She was returning to her home and to the parents who were so dear to her, yet she was filled with an overwhelming sense of loneliness and loss. For all her days there would be only memories, like flowers pressed between the leaves of a book, their colors faded, their perfume gone, pale husks of the beauty and the glamour of love. 

Over and over in her mind had rung the changes of her own argument and Lancelot's. She had taken the

FLIGHT'S END 

301 step that seemed to her the right one, yet she could not justify, to her own satisfaction, any course which ignored their right to live their lives in their own way. 

Her longing for Lancelot was almost intolerable. How could she go on, she wildly demanded of herself, knowing that she was never to see him again? How could she bear this aching of her heart, so intense that it was almost physical pain? She might go on living, she thought despairingly, to be very, very old, like that ancient man of whom Gerda had spoken. There might be years and years of this emptiness, of this bleak desolation, which was far worse than death. 

She blinked fast to keep back the tears which brimmed her eyes as that mournful picture shaped itself. Gerda was calling. She must go back, hide her wretchedness, pretend an interest in passing events, smile at the gaiety of the wedding group and be glad that for others, if not for herself, the prophecy of the white hawthorne and apple blossoms had been realized, bringing them "True Lovers' Year." FLIGHT'S END 

"Come on, Lance, come on! " Gerda was shouting, as if he could hear at that distance. "Ack, dey're almost ready to go. Ay bet dey vouldn't leave him come on de boat! 

The scene faded out, for Nancy. There was no throng below, calling out last messages to friends on board, no bustling dock-hands making ready for the great liner to depart, - nothing but that tall, slim figure hurrying, hurrying toward her. "Hi, Lance!" Gerda yelled, waving vigorously. "Here ve are! Look! Over here! "

He saw them. A tingle of rapture raced along Nancy's veins as for an instant their eyes met. He pressed on through the crowd and was lost to view. 

Gerda was hanging recklessly over the rail, craning her neck to follow his progress, fearful that he would not be allowed to board the ship. She gave a crow of delight when she saw him reach their deck and answer her signal. "Hello, hello, High Boy!" she greeted him. "Ain't it grand dat you could come! Ay vos so sorry ve have left vidout seeing you! " 

Nancy's heart was suffocating her with its mad beating. She stood with her hands pressed against her throat, staring with wide, apprehensive eyes at Lancelot as he passed Gerda with a gay salute and came straight to her. She had fought her battle, made her decision. She could not go through it again. She could not trust herself to go through with it again.

The flurry of departure was at its height. The bride and groom, who were now near Nancy and Lancelot at the rail, were being showered with paper ribbons thrown by the gay group on the pier. They did not know when she left them, to tell Jim her wonderful news. They stood close together, Lancelot's hand covering Nancy's as it lay on the rail. 

Slowly the ship was moving away from the dock. The old man with the violin was playing a popular love song, with sentimental appeal, while the wedding party added their fresh young voices to his quavering notes. "You do like England, don't you, Deborah?" Lancelot questioned. "Oh, so much! " "No need asking if you like Bryndham Court," he teased. "You tried so hard to save it for me! " "I love it!" Nancy was looking soberly at the receding land. Not until Gerda had put it into words had she sensed what

FLIGHT'S END 

307 marriage to Lancelot would mean, - a definite break with her old life, separation from parents and friends and country, the severing of all her most precious ties. 

It was a new thought, and a most disturbing one. 

The strip of water between ship and shore was widening. Thin, faint and sweet on the air came the last strains of the song. 

Lancelot was speaking again, in his voice the vibrant tone that turned the world to magic. "You will come back with me when my leave is up, won't you, Deborah? "

She raised her eyes to his and somehow, in the radiance of his gaze, even that last shadow of doubt seemed to vanish. 66 

Whenever you are ready," she promised, "I'll come home with you."
